(\mathsf{q},\mathcal{H})

K\in\{\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{z}}}^{{0}},\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{z}}}^{{1}}\}

\mathsf{q}

Achieving robustness.

To attain robustness, we list below the differences from the fair protocol that must be carried out. The first difference is the use of a robust variant of
\mathsf{jsnd}
. Second, in input sharing protocol, where
\mathsf{x}_{1}
is held by only garbler
P_{0}
, a corrupt
P_{0}
may refrain from providing
P_{1}
with the correct key (sent as the opening information for the commitment). To ensure robustness, if
P_{1}
fails to receive the correct key from
P_{0}
, we let
P_{1}
complain to all parties about this inconsistency by sending an inconsistency bit. All parties exchange this inconsistency bit among themselves and agree on the majority value. If all parties agree on the presence of inconsistency, then
P_{0},P_{1}
are identified to be in conflict, and
\mathsf{TTP}=P_{2}
is set to carry out the rest of the computation.
Finally, to ensure a robust reconstruction, the following approach is taken. Observe that the fair reconstruction provides robustness as long as evaluator
P_{1}
is honest. When none of the garblers obtains the output in the fair protocol, it is guaranteed that evaluator
P_{1}
is corrupt. Thus, in such a scenario, all parties take
P_{1}
to be corrupt and proceed with
P_{0}
as the
\mathsf{TTP}
.

5.4
Security proofs

Without loss of generality, we prove the security of our robust framework. The case for fairness follows similarly, and we omit its details. We provide proofs in the
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{setup}},\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{jsnd}$}}
-hybrid model, where
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{setup}}
(§
2.5.1
),
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{jsnd}$}}
(Fig.
5.18
) denote the ideal functionality for the shared-key setup and
\mathsf{jsnd}
, respectively.

The strategy for simulating the computation of function
f
(represented by a circuit
\mathsf{Ckt}
) is as follows: Simulation begins with the simulator emulating the shared-key setup (
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{setup}}
) functionality and giving the respective keys to the adversary. This is followed by the input sharing phase in which
\mathcal{S}
computes the input of
\mathcal{A}
, using the known keys, and sets the honest parties’ inputs to be used in the simulation to
0
.
\mathcal{S}
invokes the ideal functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{GOD}}
on behalf of
\mathcal{A}
using the extracted input and obtains the output
\mathsf{y}
.
\mathcal{S}
now knows the inputs of
\mathcal{A}
and can compute all the intermediate values for each building block.
\mathcal{S}
proceeds with simulating each of the building blocks in the topological order. We provide the simulation for the case for corrupt
P_{0},P_{1}
and
P_{3}
. The case for corrupt
P_{2}
is similar to that of
P_{1}
.

For modularity, we provide the simulation steps for each building block separately. Carrying out these blocks in the topological order yields the simulation for the entire computation. If a
\mathsf{TTP}
is identified during the simulation, the simulator stops and returns the function output to the adversary on behalf of the
\mathsf{TTP}
as per
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{jsnd}$}}
.

Ideal
\mathsf{jsnd}
Functionality

The ideal
\mathsf{jsnd}
functionality for fairness security appears in Fig.
5.17
and that for the robust setting appears in Fig.
5.18
.

\justify

\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{jsnd}$}}
interacts with the parties in
\mathcal{P}
and the adversary
\mathcal{S}
.

Step 1:

\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{jsnd}$}}
receives
(\mathsf{Input},\mathsf{v}_{s})
from senders
P_{s}
for
s\in\{i,j\}
,
(\mathsf{Input},\bot)
from receiver
P_{k}
and fourth party
P_{l}
. While sending the inputs, the adversary is also allowed to send a special
\mathtt{abort}
command.

Step 2:

Set
\mathsf{msg}_{i}=\mathsf{msg}_{j}=\mathsf{msg}_{l}=\bot
.

Step 3:

If
\mathsf{v}_{i}=\mathsf{v}_{j}
, set
\mathsf{msg}_{k}=\mathsf{v}_{i}
. Else, set
\mathsf{msg}_{k}=\mathtt{abort}
.

Step 4:

Send
(\mathsf{Output},\mathsf{msg}_{s})
to
P_{s}
for
s\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

:
Ideal functionality for
\mathsf{jsnd}
in
Tetrad

interacts with the parties in
\mathcal{P}
and the adversary
\mathcal{S}
.

Step 1:

\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{jsnd}$}}
receives
(\mathsf{Input},\mathsf{v}_{s})
from senders
P_{s}
for
s\in\{i,j\}
,
(\mathsf{Input},\bot)
from receiver
P_{k}
and fourth party
P_{l}
, while it receives
(\mathsf{select},\mathsf{ttp})
from
\mathcal{S}
. Here
\mathsf{ttp}
is a boolean value, with a

\mathsf{TTP}=P_{l}
should be established.

Step 2:

If
\mathsf{v}_{i}=\mathsf{v}_{j}
and
\mathsf{ttp}=0
, or if
\mathcal{S}
has corrupted
P_{l}
2
2

2

This condition is used to capture the fact that a corrupt
P_{l}
cannot create an inconsistency in
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{jsnd}$}}
since the parties actively involved in
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{jsnd}$}}
would be honest
, set
\mathsf{msg}_{i}=\mathsf{msg}_{j}=\mathsf{msg}_{l}=\bot,\mathsf{msg}_{k}=\mathsf{v}_{i}
and go to
Step 4
.

Step 3:

Else, set
\mathsf{msg}_{i}=\mathsf{msg}_{j}=\mathsf{msg}_{k}=\mathsf{msg}_{l}=P_{l}
.

Step 4:

Send
(\mathsf{Output},\mathsf{msg}_{s})
to
P_{s}
for
s\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

:
Ideal functionality for robust
\mathsf{jsnd}
in
Tetrad
.

Sharing Protocol (
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
, Fig.
5.2
)

During the preprocessing,
\mathcal{S}_{\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}}^{P_{0}}
emulates
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{setup}}
and gives the respective keys to
\mathcal{A}
. The values commonly held with
\mathcal{A}
are sampled using the respective keys, while others are sampled randomly. The details for the online phase are provided next. We omit the simulation for corrupt
P_{3}
as it is similar to that of
P_{1},P_{2}
.

\justify

Online:

–

If dealer is
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}}^{P_{0}}
receives
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}
from
\mathcal{A}
on behalf of
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
. If the received values are consistent,
\mathcal{S}_{\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}}^{P_{0}}
computes
\mathcal{A}
’s input
\mathsf{v}
as
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\right]_{1}-\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\right]_{2}-\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\right]_{3}
, else sets
\mathsf{v}
as the default value. It invokes
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{GOD}}
on input
(\mathsf{Input},\mathsf{v})
to obtain the function output
\mathsf{y}
.

–

If dealer is
P_{1},P_{2}
or
P_{3}
, nothing to simulate as
P_{0}
doesn’t receive any value during the protocol.

:
Simulator
\mathcal{S}_{\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}}^{P_{0}}
for corrupt
P_{0}

\justify

Online:

–

If dealer is
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}}^{P_{1}}
receives
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}
from
\mathcal{A}
on behalf of
P_{2},P_{3}
. If the received values are consistent,
\mathcal{S}_{\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}}^{P_{1}}
computes
\mathcal{A}
’s input
\mathsf{v}
as
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\right]_{1}-\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\right]_{2}-\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\right]_{3}
, else sets
\mathsf{v}
as the default value. It invokes
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{GOD}}
on input
(\mathsf{Input},\mathsf{v})
to obtain the function output
\mathsf{y}
.

–

If dealer is
P_{0},P_{2}
or
P_{3}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}}^{P_{1}}
sets
\mathsf{v}=0
and performs the protocol steps honestly.

Figure 5.20
:
Simulator
\mathcal{S}_{\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}}^{P_{1}}
for corrupt
P_{1}

Shares unknown to
\mathcal{A}
are sampled randomly in the simulation, whereas in the real protocol, they are sampled using the pseudorandom function (PRF). The indistinguishability of the simulation thus follows by a reduction to the security of the PRF. The same holds for the rest of the blocks.

The simulation for the joint sharing protocol (
\Pi_{\mathsf{JSh}}
) is similar to that of the sharing protocol. The protocol’s design is such that the simulator will always know the value to be sent as part of the joint sharing protocol. The communication is constituted by
\mathsf{jsnd}
calls and is emulated according to the simulation of
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{jsnd}$}}
.

Multiplication Protocol (
\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}
in
Tetrad-R
II
)

\justify

Reconstruction Protocol (
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
, Fig.

)

Using the input of
\mathcal{A}

on behalf of
\mathcal{A}
and obtains the function output
\mathsf{y}
in clear.
\mathcal{S}_{\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}}
calculates the missing share of
\mathcal{A}
using
\mathsf{y}
and the other shares. The missing share is then communicated to
\mathcal{A}
by emulating the
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{jsnd}$}}
functionality.

Chapter 6
ABY2.0
: 2PC Semi-honest Protocols

This chapter provides details for the Layer I blocks of our 2PC framework
ABY2.0
. Some of the results in this chapter resulted in a publication at USENIX Security’21
[
113
]

1

This is joint work with Thomas Schneider and Hossein Yalame of TU Darmstadt. All co-authors contributed to the fruitful discussions that resulted in this publication. Ajith Suresh designed the new sharing scheme for two-party computation, provided new conversions between different MPC protocols, and benchmarked the protocols. Hossein Yalame designed the new circuits for parallel-prefix adder, comparison, and equality test based on multi-input AND gates and provided the depth-optimized variant of AES.
.

ABY2.0
with passively secure 2PC PPML framework of

]

.

6.1
Preliminaries and Definitions

In our framework, we have two parties
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},P_{2}\}
who are connected by a bidirectional synchronous channel (e.g. instantiated via TLS over TCP/IP), and a static, semi-honest adversary that can corrupt at most one party. This framework is similar to that of the three-party framework
ASTRA
except for the absence of helper party
P_{0}
.

6.1.1
Sharing Semantics

For the arithmetic and boolean sharing, we follow masked evaluation technique, where a value
\mathsf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
is split into three shares. Two of the shares (
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2})
can be generated in the preprocessing phase independent of the value to be shared, and their sum can be interpreted as a mask (
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
). The third share, dependent on
\mathsf{v}
, can be computed in the online phase and can be treated as the masked value
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}=\mathsf{v}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
.

Table 6.2
:
Semantics for
\mathsf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
in
ABY2.0
.
†
†
nicematrix-placeholder:
NiceTabular (nicematrix)

The sharing semantics is presented in Table
6.2
, denoted by
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
, along with the semantics for
\left[\cdot\right]
-sharing. Both the sharings used are linear i.e. given sharings of
\mathsf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{v}_{m}
and public constants
c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}
, sharing of
\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}\mathsf{v}_{i}
can be computed non-interactively for an integer
m
.

Notation 6.1.1

(a) For the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
n
values
\mathsf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{a}_{n}
,
\gamma_{\mathsf{a}_{1}\ldots\mathsf{a}_{n}}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}}
and
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}_{1}\ldots\mathsf{a}_{n}}{}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}}{}
(b) We use superscripts
{\bf B}
, and
{\bf G}
to denote sharing semantics in boolean, and garbled world, respectively–
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
,
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
. We omit the superscript for arithmetic world.

Sharing semantics for boolean sharing over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is similar to arithmetic sharing except that addition is replaced with XOR. The semantics for garbled sharing are described in §
6.3
with the relevant context.

6.1.2
Oblivious Transfer (OT)

In a 1-out-of-
n
Oblivious Transfer
[
70
,
104
]
(OT) over
\ell
-bit messages, the sender
S
inputs
n
messages
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
each of length
\ell
bits, while the receiver
R
inputs the choice
c\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
R
receives
x_{c}
as output while
S
receives
\bot
as output. The privacy guarantee is that
S
learns nothing about
c
, while
R
learns nothing about the inputs of
S
other than
x_{c}
. We use
\mathsf{n\text{-}OT}^{m}_{\ell}
to denote
m
instances of 1-out-of-
n
OT on
\ell
bit inputs.

OT is a fundamental building block for MPC
[
80
]
and requires expensive public-key cryptography
[
70
]
. The technique of OT Extension
[
71
,
9
,
81
,
111
]
allows us to generate many OTs from a small number (equal to the security parameter) of base OTs at the expense of symmetric-key operations alone. This reduces the cost of OT mainly to highly efficient symmetric-key primitives. Concretely, the OT Extension of
[
9
]
generates around 1 million
\mathsf{2\text{-}OT}^{1}_{\ell}
per second with passive security. An orthogonal line of work considered pre-computation of OT
[
11
]
, where all the cryptographic operations can be shifted to a setup phase, independent of the function to be evaluated. This technique enables a very efficient online phase for protocols that use OT. In the semi-honest setting, the state-of-the-art solution for OT extension
[
9
]
has communication
\kappa+2\ell
bits per OT for
\mathsf{2\text{-}OT}^{1}_{\ell}
where
\kappa
denotes the computational security parameter.

A correlated OT (
\mathsf{cOT}
)
[
9
]
is a variant of the traditional OT where the sender’s input messages are correlated. In a
\mathsf{cOT}
, the sender inputs a correlation function
f()
and obtains the message pair (
x_{0}\in_{R}\{0,1\}^{\ell},x_{1}=f(x_{0})
) as the output. The receiver, on the other hand, inputs her choice
c
and obtains
x_{c}
as output. We use
\mathsf{cOT}^{m}_{\ell}
to denote
m
instances of 1-out-of-2 correlated OT on
\ell
bit inputs. In the semi-honest setting,
\mathsf{cOT}^{1}_{\ell}
has communication
\kappa+\ell
bits
[
9
]
.

6.1.3
Homomorphic Encryption (HE)

The homomorphic property allows us to compute a ciphertext from a set of ciphertexts such that the plaintext underlying the former is a function of the underlying plaintexts of the latter. Towards this, one party called client generates a key-pair
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})
for the HE scheme and sends
\mathsf{pk}
to the other party called server. To perform a secure computation operation, the client encrypts its data using
\mathsf{pk}
and sends this to the server. Now the server can locally compute the ciphertext corresponding to the operation and return the encrypted result to the client. The client can now decrypt the received ciphertext using her private key
\mathsf{sk}
. An
additively HE
allows us to generate the ciphertext corresponding to the sum of the underlying plaintexts by doing operations on the ciphertexts. Prominent examples of additively HE schemes are Paillier
[
108
]
, DGK
[
47
]
and RLWE-AHE
[
119
]
. On the other hand, fully homomorphic encryption schemes allow arbitrary computations under the encryption but are less efficient. See
[
3
]
for a more detailed description.

6.2
Arithmetic / Boolean 2PC

This section covers the details of our 2PC semi-honest protocol
ABY2.0
over an arithmetic ring
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
. The protocol primarily consists of the following primitives – i) Sharing ( §
6.2.1
), ii) Multiplication ( §
6.2.2
), and iii) Reconstruction ( §
6.2.3
).

6.2.1
Sharing

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
(Fig.
6.1
) enables
P_{i}
to generate
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-share of a value
\mathsf{v}
. During the preprocessing phase,
\mathsf{\lambda}
-shares are sampled non-interactively using the pre-shared keys (cf. §
2.5.1
) in a way that
P_{i}
will get the entire mask
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
. During the online phase,
P_{i}
computes
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}=\mathsf{v}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
and sends to
P_{1},P_{2}
.
For the special case when parties want to generate
\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket
in the preprocessing, the protocol can be made non-interactive. W.l.o.g. consider the case when
P_{i}=P_{1}
. Parties set
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}=0
.
P_{1},P_{2}
sample
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
non-interactively while
P_{1}
sets
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}=-(\mathsf{v}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2})
. The case for
P_{i}=P_{2}
is similar.

Input(s):

P_{i}:\mathsf{v}
,
Output:

\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket
.

\justify

Preprocessing:

Sample as follows:
P_{i},P_{1}:\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}
,
P_{i},P_{2}:\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
.

\justify

Online:

P_{i}
computes
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}=\mathsf{v}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
and sends to
P_{1},P_{2}
.

Figure 6.1
:
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing of a value
\mathsf{v}
by party
P_{i}
in
ABY2.0
.

Lemma 6.2.2

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
(Fig.
6.1
) requires a communication of at most
\ell
bits and
1
round in the online phase.

Proof 6.2.3
.

The preprocessing of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
is non-interactive as the parties sample non interactively using key setup
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{Key}}
(§
2.5.1
). In the online phase,
P_{i}
sends
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}
to either
P_{1}
or
P_{2}
(depending upon
P_{i}
) resulting in 1 round and communication of
\ell
bits.

6.2.1.1
Joint Sharing

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{JSh}}
enables parties
P_{1},P_{2}
to generate
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-share of a value
\mathsf{v}
known to both of them non-interactively. For this, parties set
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}=0
and
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}=\mathsf{v}
.

6.2.2
Multiplication

Given the shares of
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
, the goal of the multiplication protocol is to generate shares of
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{ab}
. The protocol is designed such that
P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
obtain
\mathsf{z}^{i}
in the online phase such that
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{z}^{1}+\mathsf{z}^{2}
. Parties then compute
\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket
as
\llbracket\mathsf{z}^{1}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{z}^{2}\rrbracket
to obtain the final output.

Online

Note that,

\displaystyle\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{ab}=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}})=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{ab}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}+\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{\footnotesize{(cf. notation\penalty\ \ref{notation:2pcSconcise})}}

(6.1)

Let
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{z}_{1}+\mathsf{z}_{2}
, where
\mathsf{z}_{1}
and
\mathsf{z}_{2}
can be computed respectively by
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.

\displaystyle P_{1}:\mathsf{z}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{ab}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}+\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}\right]_{1}

\displaystyle P_{2}:\mathsf{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}+\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}\right]_{2}

(6.2)

During preprocessing, parties rely on
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
to generate an additive sharing (
\left[\cdot\right]
) of
\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}
. We note that Turbospeedz
[
17
]
achieves same online cost as that of ours, but with a more expensive preprocessing. We provide more details in §
6.2.5
.

\mathsf{isTr}
is a bit denoting whether truncation is required (
\mathsf{isTr}=1
) or not (
\mathsf{isTr}=0
).

Input(s):

\llbracket\mathsf{a}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
.

Output:

\llbracket\mathsf{o}\rrbracket
where
\mathsf{o}=\mathsf{z}^{\mathsf{t}}
if
\mathsf{isTr}=1
and
\mathsf{o}=\mathsf{z}
if
\mathsf{isTr}=0
and
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{ab}
.

\justify

Preprocessing:

Execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
on
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}\right]
and
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]
to generate
\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}\right]
.

\justify

Online:

1.

Compute:
P_{1}:\mathsf{z}_{1}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{ab}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}+\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}\right]_{1},\penalty\ \penalty\ P_{2}:\mathsf{z}_{2}=-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}+\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}\right]_{2}

2.

If
\mathsf{isTr}=1
,
P_{i}
sets
\mathsf{p}_{i}=\mathsf{z}_{i}^{\mathsf{t}}
, else
\mathsf{p}_{i}=\mathsf{z}_{i}
where
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}(P_{i},\mathsf{p}_{i})
to generate
\llbracket\mathsf{p}_{i}\rrbracket
.

3.

Compute
\llbracket\mathsf{o}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{p}_{1}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{p}_{2}\rrbracket
. Here
\mathsf{o}=\mathsf{z}^{\mathsf{t}}
if
\mathsf{isTr}=1
and
\mathsf{z}
otherwise.

Figure 6.2
:
Multiplication with / without truncation in
ABY2.0
.

Preprocessing

We now provide the details for instantiating
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
using two of the well-known primitives: i) Oblivious Transfer (OT) as used in
[
51
,
78
]
and ii) Homomorphic Encryption (HE) as used in
[
68
,
48
,
119
]
. These two approaches have been rallied against each other in terms of practical efficiency in the past, and fair competition is still going on. In our work, we make only black-box access to these primitives, and hence any improvement in any of them will directly impact the overall efficiency of the setup phase of our protocols.

Note that
\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}=(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2})(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2})=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}
. Here
P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
can locally compute
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{i}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{i}
and hence the problem reduces to computing
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}
and
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}
.

OT based
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
:

In our OT-based approach, we use Correlated OTs (
\mathsf{cOT}
)
[
9
]
where the sender inputs a correlation function
f(\cdot)
to
\mathsf{cOT}
and obtains
(m_{0},m_{1})
, where
m_{0}
is a random element and
m_{1}=f(m_{0})
. We use
\mathsf{cOT}^{n}_{\ell}
to represent
n
parallel instances of 1-out-of-2 Correlated OTs on
\ell
bit input strings.

To compute
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}\right]
, the parties execute
\mathsf{cOT}^{\ell}_{\ell}
with
P_{1}
being the sender and
P_{2}
being the receiver. For the
j
-th instance of
\mathsf{cOT}
where
j\in\{0,\ldots,\ell-1\}
,
P_{1}
inputs the correlation
f_{j}(x)=x+2^{j}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}
and obtains
(m_{j,0}=r_{j},m_{j,1}=r_{j}+2^{j}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1})
.
P_{2}
inputs choice bit
b_{j}
as the
j
-th bit of
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}
and obtains
m_{j,b_{j}}
as output. Now the
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares are defined as
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}\right]_{1}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}(-r_{j})
and
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}\right]_{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}m_{j,b_{j}}
. Computation of
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}
proceeds similarly with the role of the parties reversed.

In the OT-based approach
[
51
,
78
]
, the technique of OT extension
[
9
,
81
,
111
]
can be used. One instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
requires two instances of
\mathsf{cOT}^{\ell}_{\ell}
where each instance has communication
\ell(\kappa+\ell)
bits. Over a
64
-bit ring, this corresponds to
3072
bytes.
Recently,
[
26
]
came up with a very efficient OT extension technique named Silent OT Extension which claims to outperform state-of-the-art solutions for performing
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
. Since our protocol makes black-box calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
, it can directly benefit from the performance improvements of
[
26
]
.

HE-based
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
:

In a HE based solution,
P_{1}
, using its public key
\mathsf{pk_{1}}
, encrypts its messages
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}
in independent ciphertexts and sends the ciphertexts to
P_{2}
. In parallel,
P_{2}
computes the ciphertexts corresponding to
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}
and a random element
\mathsf{r}\in_{R}\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
using
\mathsf{pk_{1}}
. Upon receiving the ciphertexts from
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
computes the ciphertext corresponding to
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}-\mathsf{r}
using the homomorphic property of the underlying HE.
P_{2}
then sends encryption of
\mathsf{v}
to
P_{1}
who then decrypts it using its secret key
\mathsf{sk_{1}}
. Note that (
\mathsf{v}
,
\mathsf{r}
) forms an additive sharing of the desired value:
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}=\mathsf{v}+\mathsf{r}
.

Recently, Ring LWE-based AHE
[
119
]
was shown to outperform the solutions based on OT for generating multiplication triples. The authors observed that the plaintext space is much larger than the range of the values being encrypted. Thus they used the technique of
ciphertext packing
, using Microsoft SEAL library, where ciphertexts corresponding to multiple plaintexts are packed into a single ciphertext. This optimizes the number of ciphertexts being sent back and the number of decryptions on
P_{1}
’s side. In
[
119
]
, the amortized communication cost for performing one instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
over a
64
-bit ring with a security level of 128 bits is
448
bytes, which is a
7\times
improvement over the best OT-based solutions
[
51
]
available at that time.

Lemma 6.2.4

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}
(Fig.
6.2
) (in
ABY2.0
) requires
2\ell(\kappa+\ell)
bits of communication in the preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

Proof 6.2.5
.

During the preprocessing, as part of
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
, we use 2 instances of correlated OTs (
\mathsf{cOT}
)
[
9
]
which incur a communication of
\ell+\kappa
bits per
\mathsf{cOT}
on
\ell
-bit strings, where
\kappa
is the computational security parameter. During the online phase, each of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
executes one instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
and the cost follows from Lemma
6.2.2
.

6.2.2.1
Truncation

To accommodate truncation, following
ASTRA
,
P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
locally truncates
\mathsf{z}_{i}
before executing the sharing in the online of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}
(Fig.
6.2
). The correctness follows from
[
102
]
.

6.2.2.2
Multiplication with constant

Multiplication by a constant in MPC is typically local. Given constant
\alpha
and
\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket
, the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of the product
\mathsf{y}=\alpha\mathsf{v}
can be locally computed as per (
6.3
).

\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}}=\alpha\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{u}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{1}=\alpha\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{2}=\alpha\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}

(6.3)

However, in FPA, we need to perform a truncation on the output. Let
\alpha\mathsf{v}=\beta^{1}+\beta^{2}
where
\beta^{1}=\alpha.(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1})
and
\beta^{2}=-\alpha.\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
.
P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
locally truncates
\beta^{i}
and executes the sharing
protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
on the truncated value. Parties locally compute
\llbracket\alpha\mathsf{v}\rrbracket=\llbracket\beta^{1}\rrbracket+\llbracket\beta^{2}\rrbracket
to obtain the final result.

6.2.3
Reconstruction

\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}(\mathcal{P},\mathsf{v})
enables parties to compute
\mathsf{v}
, given its
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-share. For this,
P_{1}
sends
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}
to
P_{2}
and
P_{2}
sends
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
to
P_{1}
. Parties locally compute
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
. Reconstruction towards a single party can be viewed as a special case.

Lemma 6.2.6

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
requires a communication of
2\ell
bits and
1
round.

6.2.4
Multi-input Multiplication

6.2.4.1
3-input multiplication

To compute
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{abc}
, note that

\displaystyle\mathsf{z}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{abc}=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{c}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{c}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{abc}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{ac}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{bc}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{ab}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{c}}+\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}\gamma_{\mathsf{bc}}+\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}\gamma_{\mathsf{ac}}+\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{c}}\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}-\gamma_{\mathsf{abc}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{\footnotesize{(cf. notation\penalty\ \ref{notation:2pcSconcise})}}

(6.4)

Similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}
, parties rely on
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
to generate an additive sharing (
\left[\cdot\right]
) of
\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}},\gamma_{\mathsf{bc}}
and
\gamma_{\mathsf{ac}}
. Parties then generate
\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{abc}}\right]
using another instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
with inputs
\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}
and
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{c}}
.

Lemma 6.2.7

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult3}}
(in
ABY2.0
) requires
8\ell(\kappa+\ell)
bits of communication in the preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

Proof 6.2.8
.

The preprocessing involves four instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
each costing a communication of
2\ell(\kappa+\ell)
bits.
The online phase is similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}
and the costs follow from Lemma
6.2.4
.

6.2.4.2
4-input multiplication

For the case of 4-input multiplication with
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{abcd}
, note that

\displaystyle\mathsf{z}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{abcd}-\mathsf{r}=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{c}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{c}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{d}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{abcd}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{abc}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{d}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{abd}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{c}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{acd}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{bcd}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}+\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{ab}}\gamma_{\mathsf{cd}}+\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{ac}}\gamma_{\mathsf{bd}}+\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{ad}}\gamma_{\mathsf{bc}}+\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{bc}}\gamma_{\mathsf{ad}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{bd}}\gamma_{\mathsf{ac}}+\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{cd}}\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}\gamma_{\mathsf{bcd}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}\gamma_{\mathsf{acd}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{c}}\gamma_{\mathsf{abd}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{d}}\gamma_{\mathsf{abc}}+\gamma_{\mathsf{abcd}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{\footnotesize{(cf. notation\penalty\ \ref{notation:2pcSconcise})}}

(6.5)

Here the parties need to generate
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of
\gamma_{\mathsf{ab}},\gamma_{\mathsf{ac}},\gamma_{\mathsf{ad}},\gamma_{\mathsf{bc}},\gamma_{\mathsf{bd}},\gamma_{\mathsf{cd}},\gamma_{\mathsf{abc}},\gamma_{\mathsf{abd}},\gamma_{\mathsf{acd}},\gamma_{\mathsf{bcd}}
and
\gamma_{\mathsf{abcd}}
. This is computed similarly as in 3-input multiplication and the protocol is denoted as
\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult4}}
.

Lemma 6.2.9

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult4}}
(in
ABY2.0
) requires
22\ell(\kappa+\ell)
bits of communication in the preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

[

]

We compare our multi-input AND gate protocols with
[
52
]
for two, three and four inputs.

52
]
proposed two variants – i) OP-LUT - optimized online communication of
2N
bits, and ii) SP-LUT - optimized total communication of
2\kappa+2^{N}
bits. The concrete details are given in Table
6.3
.

6.2.5
Comparison with Turbospeedz
[
17
]
and
[
106
]

Here, we compare our 2PC protocol with Turbospeedz
[
17
]
and
[
106
]
.

6.2.5.1
Comparison with Turbospeedz
[
17
]

For the 2-input multiplication, Turbospeedz
[
17
]
presented a protocol that reduces the online communication of SPDZ-style protocols from
4
to
2
ring elements using a function-dependent preprocessing. Turbospeedz first executes a SPDZ-like preprocessing where random multiplication triples are generated. These triples are then associated with the multiplication gates using additional values that they call
“external values”
(cf.
[
17
]
, §3.2). On the contrary, we obtain the preprocessing data directly and hence save communication of 4 ring elements and storage of 5 ring elements compared with Turbospeedz. Table
6.4
provides the communication and storage required for the 2-input multiplication protocol of ABY
[
51
]
, Turbospeedz
[
17
]
and
ABY2.0
.

†
†
nicematrix-placeholder:
NiceTabular (nicematrix)

:
Comparison of
ABY2.0
with ABY
[
51
]
and Turbospeedz
[
17
]
in terms of storage and communication for a single multiplication. All values are given in bits.
|\mathsf{Triple}|
denotes the communication required to generate a multiplication triple. Best values for the online phase are marked in bold.

For the multi-input multiplication (fan-in of
N
), the tree-based method (multiplying
N
elements by taking two at a time) requires
\log_{2}(N)
rounds for both ABY
[
51
]
and Turbospeedz
[
17
]
, while it requires communication of
4(N-1)
ring elements for ABY and
2(N-1)
elements for Turbospeedz in the online phase.

6.2.5.2
Comparison with
[
106
]

Recently,
[
106
]
proposed round-efficient solutions for multi-input multiplication using a preprocessing for which the communication cost grows exponentially with the fan-in of the multiplication gate. However, for an
N
-input multiplication,
[
106
]
requires an online communication of
2N-2
ring elements. On the contrary,
ABY2.0
requires only an online communication of
2
ring elements, and the preprocessing cost remains the same as that of
[
106
]
. Note that since the preprocessing cost grows exponentially with the number of inputs to the multiplication gate,
[
106
]
considered only up to 5-input multiplication gates in their work.

\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}
when input parties are the computing parties

For the case of a two-input multiplication gate,
[
106
]
considered a special case where the input parties are the computing parties (cf.
[
106
]
, §3.4). For this case,
[
106
]
proposed a protocol for which the online communication is
2
ring elements. For the same setting, we observe that our solution results in a protocol with zero online communication. To see this, recall the online phase of our multiplication protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}
(Fig.
6.2
). The modified protocol is as follows: During the online phase, party
P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
locally computes
\mathsf{z}_{i}
such that
\mathsf{z}_{1}+\mathsf{z}_{2}=\mathsf{z}
.
Now to generate
\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket
, parties locally set
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{z}}^{1}=-\mathsf{z}_{1},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{z}}^{2}=-\mathsf{z}_{2}
and
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{z}}=0
. It is easy to see that
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{z}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{z}}^{1}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{z}}^{2}
.

6.3
Garbled World

The GC world comprises a single execution with
P_{1}
acting as garbler and
P_{2}
as the evaluator.

Input Phase

Given that the function input
\mathsf{x}
is already available as
{\llbracket\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
, the boolean values
\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{x}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{1},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}
act as the
new
inputs for the garbled computation, and garbled sharing (
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
) is generated for each of these values. The
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
-shares thus generated defines the compound sharing,
{\llbracket\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}^{\bf C}=({\llbracket\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf G})
for every input
\mathsf{x}
to the function to be evaluated via the GC. We first discuss the semantics for
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
-sharing followed by steps for generating
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf C}
-sharing.

Garbled sharing semantics

A value
\mathsf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
-shared (garbled shared) amongst
\mathcal{P}
if
P_{1}
holds
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket}^{\bf G}_{1}=\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{v}}}^{0}
and
P_{2}
holds
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket}^{\bf G}_{2}=\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{v}}}^{\mathsf{v}}
. Here,
\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{v}}}^{\mathsf{v}}=\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{v}}}^{0}\oplus\mathsf{v}\Delta
, and
\Delta
, which is known only to the garbler
P_{1}
, denotes the global offset with its least significant bit set to
1
and is same for every wire in the circuit.
A value
\mathsf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is said to be
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf C}
-shared (compound shared) if each value from
(\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2})
is
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
-shared. We write
{\llbracket\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}^{\bf C}=({\llbracket\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf G})
.

Generation of
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
and
{\llbracket\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}^{\bf C}

Protocol
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathsf{Sh}}^{\bf G}(\mathcal{P},\mathsf{v})
enables generation of
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
given
\mathsf{v}
. Garbler
P_{1}
generates
\{\mathsf{K}_{{{\mathsf{v}}}}^{\mathsf{b}}\}_{\mathsf{b}\in\{0,1\}}
which denotes the key for value
\mathsf{b}
on wire
\mathsf{v}
, following the free-XOR technique
[
82
,
84
]
. If the value
\mathsf{v}
is known to
P_{1}
, it sends
\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{v}}}^{\mathsf{v}}
to
P_{2}
. For the case when the evaluator
P_{2}
knows
\mathsf{v}
, parties engage in a
\mathsf{cOT}^{1}_{\kappa}
with
P_{1}
being the sender and
P_{2}
being the receiver. Here
P_{1}
inputs the correlation function
f_{R}(\mathsf{y})=\mathsf{y}\oplus\Delta
and obtains
(\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{v}}}^{0},\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{v}}}^{\mathsf{v}}=\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{v}}}^{0}\oplus\Delta)
while
P_{2}
inputs
\mathsf{v}
as choice bit and receives
\mathsf{K}_{{\mathsf{v}}}^{\mathsf{v}}
as the output.
To generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}^{\bf C}
,
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathsf{Sh}}^{\bf G}
is invoked for each of
\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}}
and
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}
.

Evaluation

Let
f(\mathsf{x})
be the function to be evaluated. At this point, the function input is
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf C}
-shared. This renders
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
-sharing for the input of the GC that corresponds to the function
f^{\prime}\big({\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}}},{\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}}\big)
which first combines the given boolean-shares to compute the actual input and then applies
f
on it. Let
\mathsf{GC}
denotes the garbled circuit to be sent to
P_{2}
by garbler
P_{1}
. Sending of
\mathsf{GC}
is overlapped with the key transfer (during generation of
{\llbracket\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}^{\bf C}
), to save rounds, where
P_{1}
sends
\mathsf{GC}
to
P_{2}
. On receiving the
\mathsf{GC}
,
P_{2}
evaluate it and obtain the key corresponding to the output, say
\mathsf{z}
. This generates
{\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
.

Output phase

The goal of output computation is to compute the output
\mathsf{z}
from
{\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
. To reconstruct
\mathsf{z}
towards
P_{2}
,
P_{1}
sends the least significant bit
\mathsf{p}
of
\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{z}}^{0}
, referred to as the decoding information, to
P_{2}
.
P_{2}
uses the received
\mathsf{p}
to reconstruct
\mathsf{z}
as
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{p}\oplus\mathsf{q}
, where
\mathsf{q}
denotes the least significant bit of
\mathsf{K}_{\mathsf{z}}^{\mathsf{z}}
.
P_{2}
then sends
\mathsf{z}
to
P_{1}
completing the protocol.

6.4
Security proofs

The simulation for the semi-honest 2PC case is straightforward in the
\{\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{setup}},\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{MultPre}$}}\}
-hybrid model. Here
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{setup}}
(§
2.5.1
) denotes the ideal functionality for the shared-key setup and
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{$\mathsf{MultPre}$}}
denotes the ideal functionality for the multiplication preprocessing
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
. The strategy for simulating the computation of function
f
(represented by a circuit
\mathsf{Ckt}
) is as follows. The simulation begins with the simulator emulating the shared-key setup (
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{setup}}
) functionality and giving the respective keys to the adversary
\mathcal{A}
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is given the input and output of the
\mathcal{A}
, it can compute all the intermediate values of the circuit
\mathsf{Ckt}
in clear.

For the input sharing of value
\mathsf{v}
,
\mathcal{S}
receives the
\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}
from
\mathcal{A}
on behalf of the honest parties. Similarly, for the inputs of honest parties,
\mathcal{S}
interacts with the
\mathcal{A}
with the inputs set to
0
. The simulated view is indistinguishable from the ideal view due to the privacy of the underlying sharing scheme. The linear gates involve no communication, while simulation of the multiplication protocol is straightforward. Moreover, simulation for the joint sharing (
\Pi_{\mathsf{JSh}}
) instances is similar to that of the sharing protocol. The protocol’s design is such that
\mathcal{S}
will always know the value to be sent as part of the joint sharing protocol. Finally, for the reconstruction towards
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{S}
calculates the missing share of
\mathcal{A}
using
\mathsf{y}
and the other shares. The missing share is then communicated to
\mathcal{A}
as per the reconstruction protocol.

Part II
Layer II: Building Blocks

Introduction to Layer II

In this part, we provide the details of the Layer II blocks of our three-layer architecture (Fig.
1.1
). To begin with, we provide a high-level overview of the building blocks next. Moreover, for some of the blocks, such as matrix multiplication and non-linear activation functions, the constructions are generic and instantiated with the protocols from the corresponding framework. We provide a detailed description for those blocks and omit the same from the specific chapters to avoid repetition.

Scalar Dot Product (
\Pi_{\mathsf{dotp}}
)

Scalar Dot Product forms the fundamental building block for most of the ML algorithms and hence designing efficient constructions for the same are of utmost importance. Given the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
d
-length vectors
\vec{\mathbf{a}},\vec{\mathbf{b}}
, dot product protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dotp}}
computes the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
\mathsf{z}
with
\mathsf{z}=\vec{\mathbf{a}}\odot\vec{\mathbf{b}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{d}}{\mathsf{a}}_{i}{\mathsf{b}}_{i}
. One trivial way is to invoke the multiplication protocol corresponding to each of the
d
underlying multiplications. This would result in communication linear in the vector size
d
. In this thesis, we propose methods to make the online communication independent of the vector size for all our settings. Moreover, the communication in the preprocessing phase is also made independent of the vector size for the case of three and four-party settings.

Matrix Operations and Convolutions

Linear matrix operations, such as addition of two matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
to generate matrix
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}
, can be computed by extending the scalar operations (addition, in this case) with respect to each element of the matrix. Matrix multiplication, on the other hand, can be expressed as a collection of dot products, where the element in the
i^{\text{th}}
row and
j^{\text{th}}
column of
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{A}\times\mathbf{B}
, where
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
are matrices of dimension
{\mathsf{p}}\times{\mathsf{q}}
,
{\mathsf{q}}\times{\mathsf{r}}
, respectively, can be computed as a dot product of the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
\mathbf{A}
and the
j^{\text{th}}
column of
\mathbf{B}
. Thus, computing
\mathbf{C}
of dimension
{\mathsf{p}}\times{\mathsf{r}}
requires
{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{r}}
dot products on vectors of length
\mathsf{q}
. This improves the cost of matrix multiplication over the naive approach which requires
{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{q}}{\mathsf{r}}
multiplications.

We abuse notation and follow the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing semantics for matrices. For
\mathbf{X}^{u\times v}
, we have
\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{X}\bigoplus\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1}\right]\bigoplus\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}\right]\bigoplus\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathbf{X}}^{3}\right]
for the case of active frameworks (
\textsf{SWIFT},\textsf{Tetrad}
) and
\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{X}\bigoplus\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1}\right]\bigoplus\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}\right]
for the case of passive frameworks (
\textsf{ASTRA},\textsf{ABY2.0}
) . Here
\mathsf{m}_{\mathbf{X}}
,
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1}\right]
,
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}\right]
, and
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathbf{X}}^{3}\right]
are matrices of dimension
u\times v
, and
\bigoplus
denote the matrix addition operation. Looking ahead
\mathop{\raisebox{-0.50003pt}{\large\boldmath$\ominus$}},\bigodot
will be used to denote matrix subtraction and multiplication operation, respectively.

Convolutions:
Convolutions form an important building block in several neural network architectures and can be represented as matrix multiplications, as explained in the example below. Consider a 2-dimensional convolution (
\mathsf{Conv}
) of a
3\times 3
input matrix
\mathbf{X}
with a kernel
\mathbf{K}
of size
2\times 2
. This can be represented as a matrix multiplication as follows.

\mathsf{Conv}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{x}}_{1}&{\mathsf{x}}_{2}&{\mathsf{x}}_{3}\\
{\mathsf{x}}_{4}&{\mathsf{x}}_{5}&{\mathsf{x}}_{6}\\
{\mathsf{x}}_{7}&{\mathsf{x}}_{8}&{\mathsf{x}}_{9}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{k}}_{1}&{\mathsf{k}}_{2}\\
{\mathsf{k}}_{3}&{\mathsf{k}}_{4}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{x}}_{1}&{\mathsf{x}}_{2}&{\mathsf{x}}_{4}&{\mathsf{x}}_{5}\\
{\mathsf{x}}_{2}&{\mathsf{x}}_{3}&{\mathsf{x}}_{5}&{\mathsf{x}}_{6}\\
{\mathsf{x}}_{4}&{\mathsf{x}}_{5}&{\mathsf{x}}_{7}&{\mathsf{x}}_{8}\\
{\mathsf{x}}_{5}&{\mathsf{x}}_{6}&{\mathsf{x}}_{8}&{\mathsf{x}}_{9}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{k}}_{1}\\
{\mathsf{k}}_{2}\\
{\mathsf{k}}_{3}\\
{\mathsf{k}}_{4}\end{bmatrix}

Generally, convolving a
f\times f
kernel over a
w\times h
input with
p\times p
padding using
s\times s
stride having
i
input channels and
o
output channels, is equivalent to performing a matrix multiplication on matrices of dimension
(w^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})\times(i\cdot f\cdot f)
and
(i\cdot f\cdot f)\times(o)
where
w^{\prime}=\dfrac{w-f+2p}{s}+1
and
h^{\prime}=\dfrac{h-f+2p}{s}+1
. We refer readers to
[
133
,
130
]
for more details.

Secure Comparison (
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitext}}
)

Comparing two arithmetic values is one of the major hurdles in realizing efficient secure ML algorithms. Given arithmetic shares
\llbracket\mathsf{a}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
, parties wish to check whether
\mathsf{a}>\mathsf{b}
. To compute
\mathsf{a}>\mathsf{b}
in the FPA representation, given its
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing,
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitext}}
uses the technique of extracting the most significant bit (
\mathsf{msb}
) of the value
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{b}

[
101
,
110
,
85
]
.

To compute the
\mathsf{msb}
, we use two variants - i) the communication optimized parallel prefix adder (PPA) circuit from ABY3
[
101
]
(
2(\ell-1)
AND gates,
\log\ell
depth), and ii) the round optimized bit extraction circuit from ABY2
[
113
]
. The circuit of ABY2 uses multi-input AND gates and has a multiplicative depth of
\log_{4}(\ell)
. These circuits take two
\ell
-bit values in boolean sharing as the input and output the result in boolean sharing form.

Bit to Arithmetic (
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
) / Bit Injection (
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
)

The bit to arithmetic protocol,
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
, enables computing the arithmetic sharing (
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
) of a bit
\mathsf{b}
given its boolean sharing
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
. Let
\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}
denotes the value of
\mathsf{b}\in\{0,1\}
over the arithmetic ring
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
. Then for
\mathsf{b}=\mathsf{b}_{1}\oplus\mathsf{b}_{2}
, note that
\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}=(\mathsf{b}_{1}^{\sf R}-\mathsf{b}_{2}^{\sf R})^{2}
. Similarly,
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbit2A}}
protocol computes the arithmetic sharing of
\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{b}_{2}
given the boolean sharings
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
and
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
.

Given the boolean sharing of bit
\mathsf{b}
and the arithemetic sharing of a value
\mathsf{v}
, the bit injection protocol,
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
, enables computing the arithmetic sharing corresponding to the value
\mathsf{b}\mathsf{v}
. Similarly,
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbitInj}}
computes the arithmetic sharing of
\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{b}_{2}\mathsf{v}
given
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
,
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
and
\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket
.

Equality Test (
\Pi_{\mathsf{eq}}
)

Given
\llbracket\mathsf{a}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
, the goal of the Equality Testing (
\Pi_{\mathsf{eq}}
) protocol is to check whether
\mathsf{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}\mathsf{b}
or not. An equivalent formulation of the problem
[
21
,
106
]
is to check if all the bits of
\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{b}
are
0
or not. This simple primitive is crucial in building efficient protocol for applications like Circuit-based Private Set Intersection
[
117
,
114
,
115
]
, the Table Lookup Protocol from
[
52
]
, and Data Mining
[
21
]
.

On a high level, the protocol starts with the parties computing the boolean shares of two value
\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{2}
using the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
\mathsf{a}
and
\mathsf{b}
. The values
\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{2}
are computed such that
\mathsf{v}_{1}=\mathsf{v}_{2}
implies
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{b}
. For instance, in
ASTRA
, parties set
\mathsf{v}_{1}=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{1})-(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1})
and
\mathsf{v}_{2}=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}}^{2}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}
. Note that the value
\mathsf{v}_{i}
can be locally computed by party
P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and hence can generate the boolean shares.

The parties then locally compute the boolean shares of
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{v}_{1}\oplus\mathsf{v}_{2}
. If
\mathsf{v}_{1}=\mathsf{v}_{2}
, then all the bits of
\mathsf{v}
should be
0
. Or in other words, all the bits of
\overline{\mathsf{v}}
should be
1
. This can be checked by computing an AND of all the bits of
\overline{\mathsf{v}}
. For this, the parties use 4-input AND gates and a tree structure, where
4
bits are taken at a time and the AND of them is computed in one go. This approach improves the round complexity by a factor of two (
\log_{2}(\ell)
to
\log_{4}(\ell)
for
\ell
-bit inputs) over the traditional approach using 2-input AND gates.
Parties can use the
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
protocol to generate the arithmetic equivalent of the result in shared form.

Piecewise-polynomial functions

Piece-wise polynomial functions are constructed as a series of constant polynomials
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
with public coefficients and
c_{1}<\ldots<c_{m}
such that,

\displaystyle f(y)=\begin{cases}0,&y<c_{1}\\
f_{1},&c_{1}\leq y<c_{2}\\
\ldots&\\
f_{m},&c_{m}\leq y\end{cases}

For computing
f
, we first compute a set of bits
\mathsf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{b}_{m}
such that
\mathsf{b}_{i}=1
if
y\geq c_{i}
and
0
otherwise.
f
can be computed as,
f(y)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{b}_{i}\cdot(f_{i}-f_{i-1})
, where
f_{0}=0
and
f_{m}=1
. Given the arithmetic shares (
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
) of
y
, one can obtain the boolean shares (
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
) of the bits
\mathsf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{b}_{m}
using secure comparison. The bit injection protocol is then used to compute the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
\mathsf{b}_{i}\cdot(f_{i}-f_{i-1})
. Note that
f(y)
can be viewed as a sum of
m
bit injections, and parties can add up the shares locally to obtain the final result. In
\Pi_{\mathsf{piecewise}}
, we optimize the communication further and show how to make the online communication independent of
m
.

Non-Linear Activation functions

We use the following three widely used activation functions – (i) Rectified Linear Unit (
\mathsf{ReLU}
), (ii) Sigmoid (
\mathsf{Sig}
), and (iii) Softmax (
\mathsf{softmax}
).

(i) ReLU (
\mathsf{ReLU}
):

The
\mathsf{ReLU}
function,
\mathsf{ReLU}(\mathsf{v})=\max(0,\mathsf{v})
, can be written as

\displaystyle\mathsf{ReLU}(\mathsf{v})=\begin{cases}0,&\mathsf{v}<0\\
\mathsf{v}&0\leq\mathsf{v}\end{cases}

Thus, it can be viewed as
\mathsf{ReLU}(\mathsf{v})=\bar{\mathsf{b}}\cdot\mathsf{v}
, where bit
\mathsf{b}=1
if
\mathsf{v}<0
and
0
otherwise. Here
\bar{\mathsf{b}}
denotes the complement of
\mathsf{b}
.
Given
\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket
, parties first extract the sign of
\mathsf{v}
using the bit extraction protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitext}}
. The desired result can then be obtained using an invocation of the bit injection protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
.

(ii) Sigmoid (
\mathsf{Sig}
):

The sigmoid function on value
\mathsf{v}
is given as
\ln(\frac{1}{1+e^{-v}})
. However, computing the exact function is expensive in MPC and hence, we use the following MPC-friendly variant of the Sigmoid function
[
102
,
101
]
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Sig}(\mathsf{v})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}0&\quad\mathsf{v}<-\frac{1}{2}\\
\mathsf{v}+\frac{1}{2}&\quad-\frac{1}{2}\leq\mathsf{v}\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
1&\quad\mathsf{v}>\frac{1}{2}\end{array}\right.

Thus,
\mathsf{Sig}(\mathsf{v})=1-\mathsf{b}_{1}\left(\mathsf{v}+\frac{1}{2}\right)+\mathsf{b}_{2}\left(\mathsf{v}-\frac{1}{2}\right)
, where
\mathsf{b}_{1}=1
if
\mathsf{v}<-\frac{1}{2}
and
0
otherwise, and
\mathsf{b}_{2}=1
if
\mathsf{v}<\frac{1}{2}
and
0
otherwise. Note that this can be viewed as an instance of a piecewise polynomial function.

(iii) Softmax (
\mathsf{softmax}
):

Given a set of values, the softmax function is used to compute a probability distribution among the values such that each output is between 0 and 1, and all the outputs sum up to 1. This function is used at the output layer of the neural networks in Layer III of our architecture.
For a set of
d
values,
\mathsf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{v}_{d}
, the softmax on the
i
th value
\mathsf{v}_{i}
is given as
\frac{e^{-\mathsf{v}_{i}}}{\sum_{j=1}^{d}e^{-\mathsf{v}_{j}}}
. Since the actual function is not MPC-friendly, we use the approximate variant of the same proposed by SecureML
[
102
]
and is defined as
\mathsf{softmax}(\mathsf{v}_{i})=\frac{\mathsf{ReLU}(\mathsf{v}_{i})}{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathsf{ReLU}(\mathsf{v}_{j})}
. In order to perform the division, we switch from arithmetic to garbled world and then use a division garbled circuit.

Oblivious Selection

Given
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
\mathsf{x}_{0},\mathsf{x}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
and
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
where
\mathsf{b}\in\{0,1\}
, oblivious selection (
\Pi_{\mathsf{obv}}
) enables parties to generate re-randomized
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{x}_{\mathsf{b}}
. The protocol is similar in spirit to the Oblivious Transfer primitive. Note that
\mathsf{z}
can be written as
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{b}(\mathsf{x}_{1}-\mathsf{x}_{0})+\mathsf{x}_{0}
. To compute
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing of
\mathsf{b}(\mathsf{x}_{1}-\mathsf{x}_{0})
, parties use an instance of piecewise polynomial protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{piecewise}}
with
m=1
. The
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-share of
\mathsf{z}
can then be obtained by adding the output of
\Pi_{\mathsf{piecewise}}
with
\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{0}\rrbracket
.

Maximum / Minimum among two and three values

The
\Pi_{\mathsf{max2}}
protocol is used to compute the maximum among two values
\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2}
in a secure manner given
\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{1}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{2}\rrbracket
. For this, the parties execute the secure comparison protocol on
\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{1}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{2}\rrbracket
to obtain
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}={\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{1}>\mathsf{x}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
. Note that
\Pi_{\mathsf{max2}}(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2})=\mathsf{b}\cdot(\mathsf{x}_{1}-\mathsf{x}_{2})+\mathsf{x}_{2}
and can be computed using an instance of oblivious selection protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{obv}}
. The
\Pi_{\mathsf{min2}}
protocol proceeds similarly except that
\Pi_{\mathsf{min2}}(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2})=\mathsf{b}\cdot(\mathsf{x}_{2}-\mathsf{x}_{1})+\mathsf{x}_{1}
.

Given
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2},\mathsf{x}_{3}
, the goal of the
\Pi_{\mathsf{max3}}
protocol is to find the maximum value among the three. For this, first securely compare the pairs
(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{2}),(\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3})
and
(\mathsf{x}_{2},\mathsf{x}_{3})
using the secure comparison protocol and obtain
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
and
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{3}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
respectively. Here
\mathsf{b}_{1}=1
if
\mathsf{x}_{1}>\mathsf{x}_{2}
and
0
otherwise.
\mathsf{b}_{2}
and
\mathsf{b}_{3}
are defined likewise . Now the maximum among the three, denoted by
\mathsf{y}
, can be written as
\mathsf{y}=\mathsf{b}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{b}_{2}\cdot\mathsf{x}_{1}+\overline{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\cdot\mathsf{b}_{3}\cdot\mathsf{x}_{2}+\overline{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{b}_{3}}\cdot\mathsf{x}_{3}
. To compute this, parties can use
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbitInj}}
to obtain each term in the expression for
\mathsf{y}
and can locally add them to obtain the desired result. As an optimization, we can combine the communication in the online phase corresponding to all three executions of the
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbitInj}}
protocol into one. The protocol for
\Pi_{\mathsf{min3}}
, which computes the minimum among the three values can be obtained by slightly modifying the protocol for
\Pi_{\mathsf{max3}}
. The difference lies in the expression for computing the minimum which will now be
\mathsf{y}=\overline{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\cdot\mathsf{x}_{1}+\mathsf{b}_{1}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{b}_{3}}\cdot\mathsf{x}_{2}+\mathsf{b}_{2}\cdot\mathsf{b}_{3}\cdot\mathsf{x}_{3}
.

ArgMin/ ArgMax

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{argmin}}
(Fig.
6.3
) allows parties to compute the index of the smallest element in a vector
\vec{\mathbf{x}}=(\mathsf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
of
m
elements, where
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
is
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shared, i.e. each element
\mathsf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
of
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
is
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shared. The protocol outputs a
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
-shared bit vector
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
of size
m
which has a
1
at the index associated with the minimum value in
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
, and
0
elsewhere.
We follow the standard tree-based approach
[
50
]
to recursively find the minimum value in
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
while also updating
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
to reflect the index of this smallest element. Each bit of
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
is initialized to 1. The elements of
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
are grouped into pairs and securely compared to find their pairwise minimum. Using this information,
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
is updated such that
\mathsf{b}_{j}
’s are reset to
0
for
\mathsf{x}_{j}
’s
\in\vec{\mathbf{x}}
which do not form the minimum in their respective pair; the other bits in
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
still equal
1
. The protocol recurses on the remaining elements
\mathsf{x}_{j}\in\vec{\mathbf{x}}
, which were the pairwise minimums. Eventually, only one
\mathsf{b}_{j}\in\vec{\mathbf{b}}
equals
1
, indicating that
\mathsf{x}_{j}
is the minimum, with index
j
. Computing
\Pi_{\mathsf{argmax}}
can be done similarly.
The formal protocol appears in Fig.
6.3
.

\justify

Let
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
be the bit vector of size
m
, where
m
equals the size of
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
. Parties execute the following steps in the respective preprocessing and online phases.

1.

If
m=2
, do the following.

(a)

{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}=\Pi_{\mathsf{bitext}}(\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{1}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{2}\rrbracket)
;

{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}=1\oplus{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
;

\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\Pi_{\mathsf{obv}}(\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{2}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{1}\rrbracket,{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B})
.

(b)

Return
({\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket)
.

2.

Else, if
m=3
, do the following

(a)

{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}=\Pi_{\mathsf{bitext}}(\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{1}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{2}\rrbracket)
;

\llbracket\mathsf{y}^{\prime}\rrbracket=\Pi_{\mathsf{obv}}(\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{2}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{1}\rrbracket,{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket}^{\bf B})
.

(b)

{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}=\Pi_{\mathsf{bitext}}(\llbracket\mathsf{y}^{\prime}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{3}\rrbracket)
;

\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\Pi_{\mathsf{obv}}(\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{3}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{y}^{\prime}\rrbracket,{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}^{\bf B})
.

(c)

{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}=\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}({\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}^{\bf B})
;

{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}={\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}\oplus{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
;

{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{3}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}=1\oplus{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}^{\prime}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}\oplus{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
.

(d)

Return
({\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{3}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket)
.

3.

Else, let
\vec{\mathbf{x_{1}}}=(\mathsf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{\lfloor m/2\rfloor})
and
\vec{\mathbf{x_{2}}}=(\mathsf{x}_{\lfloor m/2\rfloor+1},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
.

(a)

\big({\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},\ldots,{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{1}\rrbracket\big)=\Pi_{\mathsf{argmin}}(\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{x_{1}}}\rrbracket)
.

(b)

\big({\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{\lfloor m/2\rfloor+1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},\ldots,{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{m}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{2}\rrbracket\big)=\Pi_{\mathsf{argmin}}(\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{x_{2}}}\rrbracket)
.

(c)

{\llbracket\mathsf{d}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}=\Pi_{\mathsf{bitext}}(\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{1}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{2}\rrbracket)
;

\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\Pi_{\mathsf{obv}}(\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{2}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{1}\rrbracket,{\llbracket\mathsf{d}\rrbracket}^{\bf B})
.

(d)

{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{j}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}=\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}({\llbracket\mathsf{d}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{j}\rrbracket}^{\bf B})
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor m/2\rfloor\}
.

(e)

{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{j}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}=\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}(1\oplus{\llbracket\mathsf{d}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{d}_{j}\rrbracket}^{\bf B})
for
j\in\{\lfloor m/2\rfloor+1,\ldots,m\}
.

(f)

Return
\big({\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},\ldots,{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{m}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket\big)
.

Figure 6.3
:
Protocol to find index of smallest element in
\vec{\mathbf{x}}

To begin with, parties initialize
\mathsf{b}_{j}=1
for
\mathsf{b}_{j}\in\vec{\mathbf{b}}
by locally setting
\mathsf{m}_{{\mathsf{b}}_{j}}=1
and
\mathsf{\lambda}_{{\mathsf{b}}_{j}}^{1}=\mathsf{\lambda}_{{\mathsf{b}}_{j}}^{2}=\mathsf{\lambda}_{{\mathsf{b}}_{j}}^{3}=0
. The minimum,
\mathsf{y}_{ij}
, of two elements,
\mathsf{x}_{i},\mathsf{x}_{j}
can be computed as: one invocation of bit extraction protocol to obtain
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
-sharing of
\mathsf{b}_{ij}
, where
\mathsf{b}_{ij}=1
if
\mathsf{x}_{i}<\mathsf{x}_{j}
, and
\mathsf{b}_{ij}=0
otherwise; one invocation of oblivious selection protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{obv}}(\mathsf{x}_{j},\mathsf{x}_{i},\mathsf{b}_{ij})
, which outputs
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
\mathsf{y}_{ij}=\mathsf{x}_{j}
if
\mathsf{b}_{ij}=0
, and
\mathsf{y}_{ij}=\mathsf{x}_{i}
, otherwise.
To update
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
to reflect the pairwise minimums, we view the elements
\mathsf{x}_{j}\in\vec{\mathbf{x}}
as the leaves of a binary tree, in a bottom-up manner. For two elements in a pair, say
(\mathsf{x}_{i},\mathsf{x}_{j})
, whose pairwise minimum is
\mathsf{y}_{ij}
, we let
\mathsf{y}_{ij}
be the root node with
\mathsf{x}_{i}
as its left child and
\mathsf{x}_{j}
as its right child. Now, to update
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
, parties multiply
\mathsf{b}_{ij}
with the bits in
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
associated with the
left-reachable leaf nodes
, which comprise of all the leaf nodes (elements of
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
) that are reachable through the left child of the root.
Similarly, parties multiply
1\oplus\mathsf{b}_{ij}
with the bits in
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
associated with the
right-reachable leaf nodes
, which comprise of all the leaf nodes (elements of
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
) that are reachable through the right child of the root. Thus, if
\mathsf{b}_{ij}=1
indicating that
\mathsf{x}_{i}<\mathsf{x}_{j}
,
\mathsf{b}_{i}
remains
1
as it gets multiplied by
\mathsf{b}_{ij}=1
while
\mathsf{b}_{j}
gets reset to
0
as it gets multiplied by
1\oplus\mathsf{b}_{ij}=0
. The case for
\mathsf{b}_{ij}=0
holds for similar reasons.
Given the values
\mathsf{y}_{ij}
for the next level, and the updated
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
, the steps are applied recursively until the minimum element is obtained.

The protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{argmax}}
which allows the parties to compute the index of the largest element in a
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shared vector
\vec{\mathbf{x}}=(\mathsf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{m})
, is similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{argmin}}
with the following difference. To find the maximum among two elements
(\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{i}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{x}_{j}\rrbracket)
, parties run the bit extraction protocol to obtain
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{ij}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
as before, followed by
\Pi_{\mathsf{obv}}(\mathsf{x}_{i},\mathsf{x}_{j},\mathsf{b}_{ij})
, which outputs
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares of
\mathsf{y}_{ij}=\mathsf{x}_{i}
if
\mathsf{b}_{ij}=0
, and
\mathsf{y}_{ij}=\mathsf{x}_{j}
, otherwise. Now,
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
is updated in each level by multiplying
1\oplus\mathsf{b}_{ij}
with the bits in
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
associated with the
left-reachable leaf nodes
(as described before) and multiplying
\mathsf{b}_{ij}
with the bits in
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
associated with the
right-reachable leaf nodes
.

Mixed-world Conversions

The protocols for mixed-world conversions enable efficient transitions among the arithmetic, boolean, and garbled worlds. The efficiency lift of our framework compared to existing frameworks stands on the following useful observation– a large portion of computation in most of the MPC-based PPML framework is done over the arithmetic and boolean world; they switch to the garbled world to perform the non-linear operations (e.g. softmax) that are expensive in the arithmetic/boolean world and switch back to the arithmetic/boolean world immediately after. We leverage this phenomenon to construct
end-to-end
conversion techniques such as Arithmetic-Garbled-Arithmetic. The standard approach until now was to perform a
piece-wise
combination of
Arithmetic to Garbled
followed by a
Garbled to Arithmetic
conversion. End-to-end conversions benefit from not having to generate a full-fledged garbled-shared output after the computation. Instead, these conversions aim to produce a
“partial”
garbled-shared output that is enough to lead to an arithmetic sharing of the output. This results in end-to-end conversions of the form
“x-Garbled-x”
where x can be either arithmetic or boolean that need just a single round for our garbled world as opposed to the two in the existing works
[
101
,
38
]
.

Chapter 7
ASTRA
: Semi-honest Blocks

This chapter provides details for the Layer II blocks of our 2PC framework
ASTRA
. Details for the Layer I blocks are provided in chapter
3
.

7.1
Building Blocks

7.1.1
Dot Product (Scalar Product)

Given
\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{a}}\rrbracket,\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{b}}\rrbracket
with
|\vec{\mathbf{a}}|=|\vec{\mathbf{b}}|=\mathsf{d}
, protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dotp}}
(Fig.
7.1
) computes
\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket
such that
\mathsf{z}=(\vec{\mathbf{a}}\odot\vec{\mathbf{b}})^{\mathsf{t}}
if truncation is enabled, else
\mathsf{z}=\vec{\mathbf{a}}\odot\vec{\mathbf{b}}
. For this, we combine the partial products from the multiplication protocol across
\mathsf{d}
multiplications and communicate them in a single shot. This makes the communication cost of the dot product independent of the vector size.

\mathsf{isTr}
is a bit denoting whether truncation is required (
\mathsf{isTr}=1
) or not (
\mathsf{isTr}=0
).

Input(s):

\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{a}}\rrbracket,\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{b}}\rrbracket
.

Output:

\llbracket\mathsf{o}\rrbracket
where
\mathsf{o}=\mathsf{z}^{\mathsf{t}}
if
\mathsf{isTr}=1
and
\mathsf{o}=\mathsf{z}
if
\mathsf{isTr}=0
and
\mathsf{z}=\vec{\mathbf{a}}\odot\vec{\mathbf{b}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{d}}{\mathsf{a}}_{i}{\mathsf{b}}_{i}
.

\justify

Preprocessing:

Let
\gamma_{\vec{\mathbf{a}}\vec{\mathbf{b}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{d}}\gamma_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{b}_{i}}
.

1.

P_{0},P_{j}
sample
{\mathsf{u}}^{j}\in_{R}\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Let
{\mathsf{u}^{1}}+\mathsf{u}^{2}=\gamma_{\vec{\mathbf{a}}\vec{\mathbf{b}}}-\mathsf{r}
for
\mathsf{r}\in_{R}\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
.

2.

Party
P_{0}
: Computes
\mathsf{r}=\gamma_{\vec{\mathbf{a}}\vec{\mathbf{b}}}-{\mathsf{u}^{1}}-\mathsf{u}^{2}
. If
\mathsf{isTr}=1
, sets
\mathsf{q}=\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{t}}
, else
\mathsf{q}=\mathsf{r}
.

Executes
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}(P_{0},\mathsf{q})
to generate
\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket
.

\justify

Online:

Let
\mathsf{y}=(\mathsf{z}-\mathsf{r})-\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{d}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{b}_{i}}
.

1.

Compute:
P_{1}:\mathsf{y}_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{d}}(-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}}^{1}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{i}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{i}}^{1}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}})+{\mathsf{u}}^{1},\penalty\ \penalty\ P_{2}:\mathsf{y}_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{d}}(-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}}^{2}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{i}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{i}}^{2}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}})+{\mathsf{u}}^{2}

2.

P_{1}
sends
\mathsf{y}_{1}
to
P_{2}
, while
P_{2}
sends
\mathsf{y}_{2}
to
P_{1}
, and they locally compute
\mathsf{z}-\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{y}_{1}+\mathsf{y}_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{d}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{b}_{i}}
.

3.

P_{1},P_{2}
: If
\mathsf{isTr}=1
, set
\mathsf{p}=(\mathsf{z}-\mathsf{r})^{\mathsf{t}}
, else
\mathsf{p}=\mathsf{z}-\mathsf{r}
. Execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{JSh}}(P_{1},P_{2},\mathsf{p})
to generate
\llbracket\mathsf{p}\rrbracket
.

4.

Compute
\llbracket\mathsf{o}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{p}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{q}\rrbracket
. Here
\mathsf{o}=\mathsf{z}^{\mathsf{t}}
if
\mathsf{isTr}=1
and
\mathsf{z}
otherwise.

:

.

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dotp}}
(Fig.
7.1
) (in
ASTRA
) requires
\ell
bits of communication in preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

7.1.2
Bit Extraction

To compute most significant bit (
\mathsf{msb}
) of the value
\mathsf{v}
, note that
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}+(-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}})
as per the sharing semantics (cf. Table
3.2
).
P_{0}
generates the boolean sharing of
-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
during the preprocessing, while
P_{1},P_{2}
generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
during the online phase using joint sharing protocol. Parties compute the result by evaluating the bit extraction circuit
[
101
,
113
]
.

7.1.3
Bit to Arithmetic

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}({\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B})
(Fig.
7.2
) enables computing
\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
of a bit
\mathsf{b}
given its boolean sharing
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
. Let
\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}
denotes the value of
\mathsf{b}\in\{0,1\}
over the arithmetic ring
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
. Using our sharing semantics,

\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}})^{\sf R}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R})

(7.1)

Input(s):

{\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
,
Output:

\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}\rrbracket
.

\justify

Preprocessing:

P_{0},P_{1}
sample random
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
.
P_{0}
sends
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]_{2}=\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}-\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]_{1}
to
P_{2}
.

\justify

Online:

1.

Locally compute:

P_{1}:\mathsf{y}_{1}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R}+\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]_{1}(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ \penalty\ P_{2}:\mathsf{y}_{2}=\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]_{2}(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R})

2.

P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
executes
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
on
\mathsf{y}_{i}
to generate the respective
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares.

3.

Compute
\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{1}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{2}\rrbracket
.

Figure 7.2
:
Bit to Arithmetic conversion in
ASTRA
.

During the preprocessing,
P_{0}
generates
\left[\cdot\right]
-sharing of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}
. The online phase consists of each
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
locally computing an additive sharing of
\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}
, generating the corresponding
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing using
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
, and locally adding the shares to obtain
\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
.

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
(Fig.

) requires
\ell
bits of communication in preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
