9.1.4
Bit Injection

Given the boolean sharing of a bit
\mathsf{b}
, denoted as
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
, and the arithmetic sharing of
\mathsf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
, protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
computes
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing of
\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}\mathsf{v}
. Let
\Delta_{\mathsf{b}}
denote the value
(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R})
.
Similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}\mathsf{v}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}})^{\sf R}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{})=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}}

(9.3)

During the preprocessing,
P_{0}
generates the
\langle\cdot\rangle
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}
similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
protocol. Parties then invoke the
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultS}}
(Fig.
5.4
) on
\langle\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\rangle
to generate the
\langle\cdot\rangle
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{}
. During the online phase,
(P_{1},P_{3})
,
(P_{2},P_{3})
and
(P_{1},P_{2})
compute an additive sharing of
\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}\mathsf{v}
using (
9.1.4
) and execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{JSh}}
on them to generate the respective
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares. Parties locally add the shares to obtain the output.

Lemma 9.1.6

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
requires
6\ell+1
bits of communication in preprocessing, and
1
round and
3\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

9.1.4.1
Sum of Bit Injections

Given
m
pair of values in the shared form,
\{{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{i}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{i}\rrbracket\}_{i\in[m]}
, the goal of
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInjS}}
is to compute the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-share of
\mathsf{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{b}_{i}^{\sf R}\cdot\mathsf{v_{i}}
. For this, parties execute the preprocessing corresponding to
m
bit injections of the form
\mathsf{b}_{i}^{\sf R}\cdot\mathsf{v_{i}}
.

In the online phase, each pair of parties
(P_{1},P_{3})
,
(P_{2},P_{3})
and
(P_{1},P_{2})
locally compute an additive sharing of
\mathsf{z}_{i}
, corresponding to
\mathsf{b}_{i}^{\sf R}\cdot\mathsf{v_{i}}
first. Instead of generating the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing for each of the
m
terms, parties locally add the shares and execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{JSh}}
on the result. Concretely, parties locally compute
\mathsf{z}^{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{z}_{i}^{j}
for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
and execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{JSh}}
on
\mathsf{z}^{j}
to obtain its
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing. Finally, parties locally add up the shares similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
protocol. This results in an online communication independent of
m
.

Lemma 9.1.7

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInjS}}
requires
m\cdot(6\ell+1)
bits of communication in preprocessing, and
1
round and
3\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

9.1.4.2
Bit Injection:II

Similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
protocol, given
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
and
\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket
, protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbit2A}}
computes the arithmetic sharing of
(\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{b}_{2})^{\sf R}\mathsf{v}
. Let
\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}
,
\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}
denote the value
(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R})
,
(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R})
respectively. Using (
9.1.3.1
) and (
9.1.4
), we can write

\displaystyle(\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{b}_{2})^{\sf R}\mathsf{v}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}})^{\sf R}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}+(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}-\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}-\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}-(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}{}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}

(9.4)

During preprocessing, the
\langle\cdot\rangle
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}},\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}
and
(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}
are computed similar to that of
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbit2A}}
. Once the
\langle\cdot\rangle
-shares are generated, parties invoke the
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultS}}
(Fig.
5.4
) on
(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}
and
\langle\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\rangle
to generate the
\langle\cdot\rangle
-shares of
(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
. Similarly, parties compute
\langle\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\rangle
using two instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultS}}
.
The online phase is similar to that of
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
protocol.

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbitInj}}
requires
18\ell+2

1
round and
3\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

9.1.5
Equality Test (
\Pi_{\mathsf{eq}}
)

To check whether
\mathsf{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}\mathsf{b}
or not, given
\llbracket\mathsf{a}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
,
\Pi_{\mathsf{eq}}
proceeds with parties locally computing
\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{a}\rrbracket-\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
. According to our sharing semantics,
\mathsf{y}
can be written as
\mathsf{y}=\mathsf{y}_{1}-\mathsf{y}_{2}
where
\mathsf{y}_{1}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{3}
and
\mathsf{y}_{2}=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{2}
. Parties
(P_{1},P_{2})
and
(P_{0},P_{3})
generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
and
{\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
resepctively using the joint sharing protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{JSh}}
. Note that
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{b}
implies
\mathsf{y}_{1}=\mathsf{y}_{2}
and hence all the bits of
\mathsf{v}=\overline{(\mathsf{y}_{1}\oplus\mathsf{y}_{2})}
should be
1
. As mentioned in the introduction of Part II (
II
), parties use four input AND gates and a tree structure, where
4
bits are taken at a time and the AND of them is computed in one go.

9.2
Mixed Protocol Framework

Table
9.1
compares our sharing conversions with Trident
[
38
]
. For uniformity, we consider a function,
F
, to be computed on an
\ell
-bit inputs
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}
using a garbled circuit (GC) in the mixed framework, which gives an
\ell
-bit output
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{F(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})}
, where
\ell
denotes the ring size in bits. Let
\mathsf{G}^{\sf F}
denote the corresponding GC. In the table,
\mathsf{G}^{\sf Sn}
denotes a
n
-input garbled subtraction circuit;
\mathsf{G}^{\sf An}
denotes
n
-input garbled addition circuit;
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
denotes the garbled circuit with decoding information;
\mathsf{G}^{\sf n_{1}\times 1,\ldots,n_{m}\times m}
denotes
{\sf n_{i}}
instances of GC
\mathsf{G}^{\sf i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,{\sf m}\}
and
|\mathsf{G}^{\sf n_{1}\times 1,\ldots,n_{m}\times m}|
denotes its size.

9.2.1
Conversions involving Garbled World

Assume the GC is required to compute a function
f
on inputs
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
and let the output be
f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
. All the conversions described are for the 2 GC variant. Conversions for the 1 GC variant are straightforward, hence we omit the details.

Case I: Boolean-Garbled-Boolean

Since the inputs to the GC are available in boolean form, say
{\llbracket\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
, parties generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}^{\bf C},{\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}^{\bf C}
by invoking the garbled sharing protocol
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathsf{Sh}}^{\bf G}
.
Additionally, parties
P_{0},P_{3}
sample
\mathsf{R}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
to mask the function output,
f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
, and generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{R}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
(using the joint sharing protocol) and
{\llbracket\mathsf{R}\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
. Garblers
P_{g}\in\{P_{0},P_{2},P_{3}\}
garble the circuit which computes
\mathsf{z}=f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\oplus\mathsf{R}
, and send the GC along with the decoding information to evaluator
P_{1}
. Analogous steps are performed for evaluator
P_{2}
. Upon GC evaluation and output decoding, evaluators obtain
\mathsf{z}=f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\oplus\mathsf{R}
, and jointly boolean share
\mathsf{z}
to generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
. Parties then compute
{\llbracket f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\rrbracket}^{\bf B}={\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}\oplus{\llbracket\mathsf{R}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
.

Case II: Boolean-Garbled-Arithmetic

This is similar to
Case I
except that the circuit which computes
\mathsf{z}=f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})+\mathsf{R}
is garbled instead. Boolean sharing of
\mathsf{z}
is replaced with arithmetic, followed by computing
\llbracket f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket-\llbracket\mathsf{R}\rrbracket
.

Cases III & IV: Input in Arithmetic Sharing

The function to be computed
f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
, is modified as
f^{\prime}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{3},\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}},\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{y}}},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{3})=f(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{3},\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}}-\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{y}}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{3})
where inputs
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}
are replaced by the triples
\{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{3}\},\{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}},\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{y}}},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{3}\}
and
\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}}=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}
and
\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{y}}}=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{2}
. The circuit to be garbled thus, corresponds to the function
f^{\prime}
. Parties generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{x}}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{3}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},\allowbreak{\llbracket\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{y}}}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{3}\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
via
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathsf{Sh}}^{\bf G}
, following which, parties proceed with the rest of the computation whose steps are similar to
Case I
, and
II
, depending on the requirement on the output sharing.

9.2.2
Other Conversions

Arithmetic to Boolean

To convert arithmetic sharing of
\mathsf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
to boolean sharing, observe that
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{v}_{1}+\mathsf{v}_{2}
where
\mathsf{v}_{1}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{3}
is possessed by parties
P_{1},P_{2}
, while
\mathsf{v}_{2}=-(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2})
is possessed by parties
P_{0},P_{3}
. Thus,
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
can be computed as
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}={\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}+{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
, where
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
can be generated in the preprocessing phase, and
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
can be generated in the online phase by the respective parties executing joint boolean sharing protocol. The protocol appears in Fig.
9.3
. Boolean addition, when instantiated using the adder of ABY2.0
[
113
]
, requires
\log_{4}(\ell)
rounds.

\justify

Preprocessing:

P_{0},P_{3}
execute joint boolean sharing to generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
, where
\mathsf{v}_{2}=-(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2})
.

\justify

Online:

1.

P_{1},P_{2}
execute joint boolean sharing to generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
, where
\mathsf{v}_{1}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{3}
.

2.

Parties obtain
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}={\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}+{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
using a boolean adder circuit.

Figure 9.3
:
Arithmetic to Boolean Conversion in
Tetrad
.

Boolean to Arithmetic

To convert a boolean sharing of
\mathsf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
into an arithmetic sharing, note that

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}\mathsf{v}[i]=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}\oplus\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]})=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}\left(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}^{\sf R})\right)

where
{\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}}^{\sf R},\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}^{\sf R}
denote the arithmetic value of bits
{\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}},{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}}
over the ring
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
.
For each bit
\mathsf{v}[i]
of
\mathsf{v}
, parties generate the
\langle\cdot\rangle
-shares of
{{\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}}}^{\sf R}
in the preprocessing, similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
(Fig.
9.2
). During the online phase, additive shares for each bit
\mathsf{v}[i]
are locally computed similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
. Parties then multiply the
i
th share with
2^{i}
and locally add up to obtain an additive sharing of
\mathsf{v}
. The rest of the steps are similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
, and the formal protocol appears in Fig.
9.4
.

Let
\mathsf{v}[i]
denote the
i
th bit of
\mathsf{v}
. Let
\mathsf{p}_{i}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}^{\sf R}
, and
\mathsf{q}_{i}=\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}
.

\justify

Preprocessing:

1.

For
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,\ell-1\}
, execute the preprocessing of
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
(Fig.
9.2
) for each bit
\mathsf{v}[i]
, to generate
\langle{\mathsf{q}_{i}}\rangle=(\mathsf{q}_{i}^{1},\mathsf{q}_{i}^{2},\mathsf{q}_{i}^{3})
.

\justify

Online:

Let
\mathsf{y}_{i}=(\mathsf{v}[i])^{\sf R}
and
\mathsf{y}
denotes the arithmetic equivalent of
\mathsf{v}
.

1.

Locally compute the following:

\displaystyle P_{1},P_{3}:\mathsf{y}^{1}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}\mathsf{y}_{i}^{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}(\mathsf{p}_{i}+\mathsf{q}_{i}^{1}(1-2\mathsf{p}_{i}))

\displaystyle P_{2},P_{3}:\mathsf{y}^{2}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}\mathsf{y}_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}(\mathsf{q}_{i}^{2}(1-2\mathsf{p}_{i}))

\displaystyle P_{1},P_{2}:\mathsf{y}^{3}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}\mathsf{y}_{i}^{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}(\mathsf{q}_{i}^{3}(1-2\mathsf{p}_{i}))

2.

(P_{1},P_{3}),(P_{2},P_{3}),(P_{1},P_{2})
execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{JSh}}
on
\mathsf{y}^{1},\mathsf{y}^{2},\mathsf{y}^{3}
to generate the respective
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares.

3.

Locally compute
\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{y}^{1}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{y}^{2}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{y}^{3}\rrbracket
.

Figure 9.4
:
Boolean to Arithmetic Conversion in
Tetrad
.

We remark that the protocol
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathsf{B2A}}
can be used to efficiently generate edaBits
[
55
]
in our setting. For this, the parties non-interactively generate the boolean sharing for
\ell
-bits and perform the
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathsf{B2A}}
conversion to obtain the equivalent arithmetic value.

Chapter 10
ABY2.0
: 2PC Semi-honest Blocks

This chapter provides details for the Layer II blocks of our 2PC framework
ABY2.0
. Details for the Layer I blocks are provided in chapter
6
.

10.1
Building Blocks

10.1.1
Dot Product (Scalar Product)

Given
\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{a}}\rrbracket,\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{b}}\rrbracket
with
|\vec{\mathbf{a}}|=|\vec{\mathbf{b}}|=\mathsf{d}
, protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dotp}}
(Fig.
10.1
) computes
\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket
such that
\mathsf{z}=(\vec{\mathbf{a}}\odot\vec{\mathbf{b}})^{\mathsf{t}}
if truncation is enabled, else
\mathsf{z}=\vec{\mathbf{a}}\odot\vec{\mathbf{b}}
. The protocol is similar to the multiplication protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Mult}}
(Fig.
6.2
) except that the parties combine the partial products in the online phase across
\mathsf{d}
multiplications and communicate them in a single shot. This makes the communication cost of the dot product in the online phase independent of the vector size.

\mathsf{isTr}
is a bit denoting whether truncation is required (
\mathsf{isTr}=1
) or not (
\mathsf{isTr}=0
).

Input(s):

\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{a}}\rrbracket,\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{b}}\rrbracket
.

Output:

\llbracket\mathsf{o}\rrbracket
where
\mathsf{o}=\mathsf{z}^{\mathsf{t}}
if
\mathsf{isTr}=1
and
\mathsf{o}=\mathsf{z}
if
\mathsf{isTr}=0
and
\mathsf{z}=\vec{\mathbf{a}}\odot\vec{\mathbf{b}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{d}}{\mathsf{a}}_{j}{\mathsf{b}}_{j}
.

\justify

Preprocessing:

Execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
on
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}_{j}}\right]
and
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{j}}\right]
to generate
\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{a_{j}b_{j}}}\right]
for
j\in[\mathsf{d}]
.

\justify

Online:

1.

Locally compute:

\displaystyle P_{1}:\mathsf{z}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{d}}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a_{j}b_{j}}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}_{j}}^{1}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{j}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{j}}^{1}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}_{j}}+\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{a_{j}b_{j}}}\right]_{1})

\displaystyle P_{2}:\mathsf{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{d}}(-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{a}_{j}}^{2}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{j}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{j}}^{2}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{a}_{j}}+\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{a_{j}b_{j}}}\right]_{2})

2.

If
\mathsf{isTr}=1
,
P_{i}
sets
\mathsf{p}_{i}=\mathsf{z}_{i}^{\mathsf{t}}
, else
\mathsf{p}_{i}=\mathsf{z}_{i}
where
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}(P_{i},\mathsf{p}_{i})
to generate
\llbracket\mathsf{p}_{i}\rrbracket
.

3.

Compute
\llbracket\mathsf{o}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{p}_{1}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{p}_{2}\rrbracket
. Here
\mathsf{o}=\mathsf{z}^{\mathsf{t}}
if
\mathsf{isTr}=1
and
\mathsf{z}
otherwise.

Figure 10.1
:
Dot Product with / without Truncation in
ABY2.0
.

Lemma 10.1.1

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dotp}}
(Fig.
10.1
) (in
ABY2.0
) requires
2\mathsf{d}\ell(\kappa+\ell)
bits of communication in the preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

10.1.2
Bit Extraction

To compute most significant bit (
\mathsf{msb}
) of the value
\mathsf{v}
, note that
\mathsf{v}=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1})+(-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2})
as per the sharing semantics (cf. Table
6.2
).
P_{2}
generates the boolean sharing of
-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
during the preprocessing, while
P_{1}
generates
{\llbracket(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1})\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
during the online phase using sharing protocol. Parties compute the result by evaluating the bit extraction circuit
[
101
,
113
]
.

10.1.3
Bit to Arithmetic

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}({\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B})
(Fig.
10.2
) enables computing
\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
of a bit
\mathsf{b}
given its boolean sharing
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
. Let
\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}
denotes the value of
\mathsf{b}\in\{0,1\}
over the arithmetic ring
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
. Using our sharing semantics,

\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}})^{\sf R}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R})

(10.1)

During the preprocessing, parties interactively generate
\left[\cdot\right]
-sharing of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}
using steps similar to that of
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
. The online phase consists of each
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
locally computing an additive sharing of
\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}
, generating the corresponding
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing using
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
, and locally adding the shares to obtain
\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
.

Now we describe how to generate
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]
in the preprocessing. Since
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{1}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}
, we can write
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}=\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}-2\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}
.
Parties execute
\mathsf{cOT}^{1}_{\ell}
with
P_{1}
being the sender and
P_{2}
being the receiver.
P_{1}
inputs the correlation
f(x)=x+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}
and obtains
(m_{0}=r,m_{1}=r+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}})
.
P_{2}
inputs
c=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}
as the choice bit and obtains
m_{c}
as output. Now the
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares are defined as
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\right]_{1}=-r
and
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\right]_{2}=m_{\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}}
.

Input(s):

{\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
,
Output:

\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}\rrbracket
.

\justify

Preprocessing:

1.

Generating
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}
:

(a)

Execute
\mathsf{cOT}^{1}_{\ell}
with
P_{1}
being the sender with input
f(x)=x+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}
and
P_{2}
being the receiver with input
c=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}}^{2}
.

(b)

P_{1}
obtains
(m_{0}=r,m_{1}=r+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}})
while
P_{2}
obtains
m_{c}
.

(c)

Set
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\right]_{1}=-r
and
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\right]_{2}=m_{c}
.

2.

P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
locally computes
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]_{i}=\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{i}}-2\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\right]_{i}
.

\justify

Online:

1.

Locally compute:

P_{1}:\mathsf{y}_{1}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R}+\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]_{1}(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ \penalty\ P_{2}:\mathsf{y}_{2}=\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]_{2}(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R})

2.

P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
executes
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
on
\mathsf{y}_{i}
to generate the respective
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares.

3.

Compute
\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{1}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{2}\rrbracket
.

Figure 10.2
:
Bit to Arithmetic conversion in
ABY2.0
.

Lemma 10.1.2

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
(Fig.
10.2
) requires
\kappa+\ell
bits of communication in preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

Proof 10.1.3
.

During preprocessing, generation of
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\right]
involves one instance of
\mathsf{cOT}^{1}_{\ell}
. The online phase involves two instances of arithmetic sharing protocol in parallel, resulting in
1
round and a communication of
2\ell
bits.

10.1.3.1
Bit to Arithmetic:II

Similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
protocol, given the boolean sharings
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
, protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbit2A}}
computes the arithmetic sharing of
(\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{b}_{2})^{\sf R}
. Let
\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}
,
\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}
denote the value
(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R})
,
(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R})
respectively. Using (
10.1
), we can write

\displaystyle(\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{b}_{2})^{\sf R}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}})^{\sf R}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}+(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}

(10.2)

During preprocessing, the
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}
, and
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}
are computed similar to that of
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
(Fig.
10.2
). In parallel, parties execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
on the boolean
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}
and
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}
to generate
\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{b}_{2}}\right]=\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\right]
in boolean form. Once
\left[\gamma_{\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{b}_{2}}\right]
is generated, parties compute the
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of its arithmetic equivalent similar to that of
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
.
The online phase is similar to that of
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
protocol.

Lemma 10.1.4

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbit2A}}
requires
5\kappa+3\ell+2
bits of communication in preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

10.1.4
Bit Injection

Given the boolean sharing of a bit
\mathsf{b}
, denoted as
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
, and the arithmetic sharing of
\mathsf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
, protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
computes
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing of
\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}\mathsf{v}
. Let
\Delta_{\mathsf{b}}
denote the value
(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R})
.
Similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}\mathsf{v}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}})^{\sf R}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}})=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}}-\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}}

(10.3)

During the preprocessing, parties generate the
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}
similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
protocol.
To compute
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
, one naive method is to multiply
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}
and
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
using
\Pi_{\mathsf{MultPre}}
. The cost can be reduced further as follows. Note that

\displaystyle\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}-2\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}-2\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}-2\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}

(10.4)

Here
P_{1}
can locally compute
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}
while
P_{2}
can compute
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
. The
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares for the remaining four terms can be generated using four instances of
\mathsf{cOT}^{1}_{\ell}
similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
resulting in a communication of
4(\kappa+\ell)
bits. For instance, to compute
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}
, parties engage in an instance of
\mathsf{cOT}^{1}_{\ell}
with
P_{1}
as sender with input
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}
and
P_{2}
as receiver with choice bit
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}
.

During the online phase,
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
compute an additive sharing of
\mathsf{b}^{\sf R}\mathsf{v}
and execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
on them to generate the respective
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares.

Lemma 10.1.5

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
requires
5(\kappa+\ell)
bits of communication in preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

10.1.4.1
Sum of Bit Injections

Given
m
pair of values in the shared form,
\{{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{i}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{i}\rrbracket\}_{i\in[m]}
, the goal of
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInjS}}
is to compute the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-share of
\mathsf{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{b}_{i}^{\sf R}\cdot\mathsf{v_{i}}
. For this, parties execute the preprocessing corresponding to
m
bit injections of the form
\mathsf{b}_{i}^{\sf R}\cdot\mathsf{v_{i}}
.

In the online phase, each of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
locally compute an additive sharing of
\mathsf{z}_{i}
, corresponding to
\mathsf{b}_{i}^{\sf R}\cdot\mathsf{v_{i}}
first. Instead of generating the
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing for each of the
m
terms, parties locally add the shares and execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
on the result. Concretely, parties locally compute
\mathsf{z}^{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{z}_{i}^{j}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
and execute
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
on
\mathsf{z}^{j}
to obtain its
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-sharing. This results in an online communication independent of
m
.

Lemma 10.1.6

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInjS}}
requires
5m(\kappa+\ell)
bits of communication in preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

10.1.4.2
Bit Injection:II

Similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
protocol, given
{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{b}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
and
\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket
, protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbit2A}}
computes the arithmetic sharing of
(\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{b}_{2})^{\sf R}\mathsf{v}
. Let
\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}
,
\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}
denote the value
(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R})
,
(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R})
respectively. Using (
10.1.3.1
) and (
10.1.4
), we can write

\displaystyle(\mathsf{b}_{1}\mathsf{b}_{2})^{\sf R}\mathsf{v}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}})^{\sf R}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\oplus\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}+(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}-\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}^{\sf R}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}-\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}^{\sf R}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}-(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\Delta_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}

(10.5)

During preprocessing, the
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}},\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}
and
(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}
are computed similar to that of
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbit2A}}
. Once the
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares are generated, parties compute
\langle\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}\rangle
using steps similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
.
Using the boolean shares of
\left[\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}}\right]
computed as part of
\left[(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}\right]
and the
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
, parties compute the
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of
(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{1}}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{b}_{2}})^{\sf R}\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}
similar to protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
.
The online phase is similar to that of
\Pi_{\mathsf{bitInj}}
protocol.

Lemma 10.1.7

(Communication)
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{dbitInj}}
requires
14\kappa+12\ell+2
bits of communication in preprocessing, and
1
round and
2\ell
bits of communication in the online phase.

10.1.5
Equality Test (
\Pi_{\mathsf{eq}}
)

To check whether
\mathsf{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}\mathsf{b}
or not, given
\llbracket\mathsf{a}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
,
\Pi_{\mathsf{eq}}
proceeds with parties locally computing
\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{a}\rrbracket-\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket
. According to our sharing semantics,
\mathsf{y}
can be written as
\mathsf{y}=\mathsf{y}_{1}-\mathsf{y}_{2}
where
\mathsf{y}_{1}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{1}
and
\mathsf{y}_{2}=\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{2}
.
P_{2}
generates
{\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
during the preprocessing while
P_{1}
generates
{\llbracket\mathsf{y}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
in the online using
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
. Note that
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{b}
implies
\mathsf{y}_{1}=\mathsf{y}_{2}
and hence all the bits of
\mathsf{v}=\overline{(\mathsf{y}_{1}\oplus\mathsf{y}_{2})}
should be
1
. As mentioned in the introduction of Part II (
II
), parties use four input AND gates and a tree structure, where
4
bits are taken at a time and the AND of them is computed in one go.

10.2
Mixed Protocol Framework

Table
10.1
compares our sharing conversions with ABY
[
51
]
. For uniformity, we consider a function,
F
, to be computed on an
\ell
-bit inputs
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}
using a garbled circuit (GC) in the mixed framework, which gives an
\ell
-bit output
\mathsf{z}=\mathsf{F(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})}
, where
\ell
denotes the ring size in bits. Let
\mathsf{G}^{\sf F}
denote the corresponding GC. In the table,
\mathsf{G}^{\sf Sn}
denotes a
{\sf n}
-input garbled subtraction circuit;
\mathsf{G}^{\sf An}
denotes
{\sf n}
-input garbled addition circuit;
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
denotes the garbled circuit with decoding information;
\mathsf{G}^{\sf n_{1}\times 1,\ldots,n_{m}\times m}
denotes
{\sf n_{i}}
instances of GC
\mathsf{G}^{\sf i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,{\sf m}\}
and
|\mathsf{G}^{\sf n_{1}\times 1,\ldots,n_{m}\times m}|
denotes its size.

†

Table 10.1
:
Mixed protocol conversions of ABY
[
51
]
and
ABY2.0
.

10.2.1
Conversions involving Garbled World

Assume the GC is required to compute a function
f
on inputs
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
and let the output be
f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
.

Case I: Boolean-Garbled-Boolean

Since the inputs to the GC are available in boolean form, say
{\llbracket\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}^{\bf B},{\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
, parties generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{x}\rrbracket}^{\bf C},{\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket}^{\bf C}
by invoking the garbled sharing protocol
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathsf{Sh}}^{\bf G}
.
Additionally,
P_{1}
samples
\mathsf{R}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
to mask the function output,
f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
, and generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{R}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
and
{\llbracket\mathsf{R}\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
.
P_{g}=P_{1}
garbles the circuit which computes
\mathsf{z}=f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\oplus\mathsf{R}
, and sends the GC along with the decoding information to evaluator
P_{2}
. Upon GC evaluation and output decoding,
P_{2}
obtains
\mathsf{z}=f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\oplus\mathsf{R}
, and boolean share
\mathsf{z}
to generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
. Parties then compute
{\llbracket f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\rrbracket}^{\bf B}={\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}\oplus{\llbracket\mathsf{R}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
.

Case II: Boolean-Garbled-Arithmetic

This is similar to
Case I
except that the circuit which computes
\mathsf{z}=f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})+\mathsf{R}
is garbled instead. Boolean sharing of
\mathsf{z}
is replaced with arithmetic, followed by computing
\llbracket f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{z}\rrbracket-\llbracket\mathsf{R}\rrbracket
.

Cases III & IV: Input in Arithmetic Sharing

The function to be computed
f(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
, is modified as
f^{\prime}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{1},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2},\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{1},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{2})=f(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{1}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2},\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{1}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{2})
where inputs
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}
are replaced by the sets
\{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{x}},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{1},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{3}\}
,
\{\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{1},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{2},\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{3}\}
. The circuit to be garbled thus, corresponds to the function
f^{\prime}
. Parties generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{x}}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf G},{\llbracket\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf G}
via
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathsf{Sh}}^{\bf G}
, following which, parties proceed with the rest of the computation whose steps are similar to
Case I
, and
II
, depending on the requirement on the output sharing.
Function
f^{\prime}
can be further optimized as
f(\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2},\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{y}}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{2})
with
\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{x}}}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{x}}^{1}
and
\mathsf{\alpha}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{y}}}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{y}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{y}}^{1}
. Similar optimization can be done for the other garbling instance as well.

10.2.2
Other Conversions

Arithmetic to Boolean

To convert arithmetic sharing of
\mathsf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
to boolean sharing, observe that
\mathsf{v}=\mathsf{v}_{1}+\mathsf{v}_{2}
where
\mathsf{v}_{1}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}
is possessed by
P_{1}
, while
\mathsf{v}_{2}=-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
is possessed by
P_{2}
. Thus,
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
can be computed as
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}={\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}+{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
. For this,
P_{2}
can generate
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
in the preprocessing, and
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
can be generated in the online by
P_{1}
. The protocol appears in Fig.
10.3
. Boolean addition, when instantiated using the adder of
[
113
]
, requires
\log_{4}(\ell)
rounds.

\justify

Preprocessing:

P_{2}
generates
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
using
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
, where
\mathsf{v}_{2}=-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
.

\justify

Online:

1.

P_{1}
generates
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
using
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
, where
\mathsf{v}_{1}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}}-\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}}^{1}
.

2.

Parties obtain
{\llbracket\mathsf{v}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}={\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{1}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}+{\llbracket\mathsf{v}_{2}\rrbracket}^{\bf B}
using a boolean adder circuit.

Figure 10.3
:
Arithmetic to Boolean Conversion in
ABY2.0
.

Boolean to Arithmetic

To convert a boolean sharing of
\mathsf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
into an arithmetic sharing, note that

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}\mathsf{v}[i]=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}(\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}\oplus\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]})=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}\left(\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}^{\sf R}+\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}(1-2\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}^{\sf R})\right)

where
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{v}[i]},\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}^{\sf R}
denote the arithmetic value of bits
\mathsf{\lambda}_{\mathsf{v}[i]},\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}
over the ring
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
.
For each bit
\mathsf{v}[i]
of
\mathsf{v}
, parties generate the
\left[\cdot\right]
-shares of
\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}
in the preprocessing, similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
(Fig.
10.2
). During the online phase, additive shares for each bit
\mathsf{v}[i]
are locally computed similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
. Parties then multiply the
i
th share with
2^{i}
and locally add up to obtain an additive sharing of
\mathsf{v}
. The rest of the steps are similar to
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
, and the formal protocol appears in Fig.
10.4
.

Let
\mathsf{v}[i]
denote the
i
th bit of
\mathsf{v}
. Let
\mathsf{p}_{i}=\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}^{\sf R}
, and
\mathsf{q}_{i}=\mathsf{\lambda}^{\sf R}_{\mathsf{v}[i]}
.

\justify

Preprocessing:

1.

For
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,\ell-1\}
, execute the preprocessing of
\Pi_{\mathsf{bit2A}}
(Fig.
10.2
) for each bit
\mathsf{v}[i]
, to generate
\left[{\mathsf{q}_{i}}\right]=(\left[\mathsf{q}_{i}\right]_{1},\left[\mathsf{q}_{i}\right]_{2})
.

\justify

Online:

Let
\mathsf{y}_{i}=(\mathsf{v}[i])^{\sf R}
and
\mathsf{y}
denotes the arithmetic equivalent of
\mathsf{v}
.

1.

Locally compute:

\displaystyle P_{1}:\mathsf{y}^{1}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}\mathsf{y}_{i}^{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}(\mathsf{p}_{i}+\left[\mathsf{q}_{i}\right]_{1}(1-2\mathsf{p}_{i}))

\displaystyle P_{2}:\mathsf{y}^{2}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}\mathsf{y}_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2^{i}(\left[\mathsf{q}_{i}\right]_{2}(1-2\mathsf{p}_{i}))

2.

P_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
executes
\Pi_{\mathsf{Sh}}
on
\mathsf{y}^{j}
to generate the respective
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares.

3.

Compute
\llbracket\mathsf{y}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{y}^{1}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathsf{y}^{2}\rrbracket
.

Figure 10.4
:
Boolean to Arithmetic Conversion in
ABY2.0
.

Part III
Layer III: Applications

Introduction to Layer III

Solutions to privacy-preserving machine learning via MPC have been looked at in various works
[
102
,
101
,
133
,
110
,
37
,
38
,
85
]
. Our work considers PPML algorithms such as linear regression, logistic regression, deep neural networks (NN) and support vector machines (SVM) for benchmarking. We consider both the training and inference phases of all the algorithms except SVM. The training phase of SVM requires additional tools and primitives and is out of the scope of this work. We first give an overview of the ML algorithms, followed by the architectural details of the neural networks and support vector machine that we consider for benchmarking and the corresponding datasets.

Overview of ML algorithms

Here we provide an overview of ML algorithms and the detailed benchmarking results. The training phase in most machine learning algorithms consists of two stages– i) forward propagation, where the model computes the output, and ii) backward propagation, where the model parameters are adjusted according to the computed output and the actual output. We define one
iteration
in the training phase as one forward propagation followed by a backward propagation. We refer readers to
[
102
,
101
,
50
,
110
,
38
,
134
]
for formal details.

Linear Regression

For linear regression, one iteration can be viewed as updating the weight vector
\vec{\mathbf{w}}
using the Gradient Descent algorithm (GD). The update function for
\vec{\mathbf{w}}
is given by

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbf{w}}=\vec{\mathbf{w}}-\frac{\alpha}{B}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}\circ(\mathbf{X}_{i}\circ\vec{\mathbf{w}}-\mathbf{Y}_{i})

where
\alpha
denotes the learning rate and
\mathbf{X}_{i}
denotes a subset of batch size
B
, randomly selected from the entire dataset in the
i
th iteration. Here the forward propagation consists of computing
\mathbf{X}_{i}\circ\vec{\mathbf{w}}
, while the weight vector is updated in the backward propagation. The update function consists of a series of matrix multiplications, which can be achieved using dot product protocols. The operations of subtraction, as well as multiplication by a public constant, can be performed locally. We observe that the update function as mentioned above can be computed entirely in the arithmetic domain and can be viewed in the form of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
-shares as

\displaystyle\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{w}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{w}}\rrbracket-\frac{\alpha}{B}\llbracket\mathbf{X}_{j}^{T}\rrbracket\circ(\llbracket\mathbf{X}_{j}\rrbracket\circ\llbracket\vec{\mathbf{w}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\mathbf{Y}_{j}\rrbracket)

Logistic Regression

The iteration for the case of logistic regression is similar to that of linear regression, apart from an activation function being applied on
\mathbf{X}_{i}\circ\vec{\mathbf{w}}
in the forward propagation. We instantiate the activation function using the sigmoid function. The update function for
\vec{\mathbf{w}}
is given by

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbf{w}}=\vec{\mathbf{w}}-\frac{\alpha}{B}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}\circ\left(\mathsf{Sig}(\mathbf{X}_{i}\circ\vec{\mathbf{w}})-\mathbf{Y}_{i}\right)

One iteration of logistic regression incurs an additional cost for computing
\mathsf{Sig}(\mathbf{X}_{j}\circ{\vec{\mathbf{w}}})
as compared with that for linear regression.

Neural Networks

A neural network can be divided into various layers, where each layer contains a predefined number of nodes. These nodes are a linear function composed of a non-linear “activation” function. The nodes at the input layer or the first layer are evaluated on the input features to evaluate a neural network. The outputs from these nodes are fed as inputs to the nodes in the next layer. This process is repeated for all the layers to obtain the output. The underlying operation involved is the computation of activation matrices in all the layers. This constitutes the forward propagation phase. The backward propagation involves adjusting model parameters according to the difference between the computed and actual output and comprises computing error matrices.

Concretely, each layer comprises matrix multiplications followed by an application of the ReLU function. The maxpool layer additionally follows convolutional layers after the ReLU layer. After evaluating the layers in a sequential manner, at the output layer, we use the MPC friendly variant of the softmax activation function,
\mathsf{softmax}(u_{i})=\frac{\mathsf{ReLU}(u_{i})}{\sum_{j=1}^{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{ReLU}(u_{j})}
, proposed by SecureML
[
102
]
. To perform the division, we switch from arithmetic to garbled world and then use a division garbled circuit
[
118
]
followed by a switch back to the arithmetic world.

The network is trained using the Gradient Descent, where the forward propagation comprises of computing activation matrices for all the layers in the network. Here, the activation matrix for all the layers except the output, is defined as
\mathbf{A}_{i}=\mathsf{ReLU}(\mathbf{U}_{i})
, where
\mathbf{U}_{i}=\mathbf{A}_{i-1}\bigodot\mathbf{W}_{i}
.
\mathbf{A}_{0}
is initialized to
\mathbf{X}_{j}
, where
\mathbf{X}_{j}
is a subset of batch size B, randomly selected from the entire dataset for the j
th
iteration. The activation matrix for the output layer is defined as
\mathbf{A}_{m}=\mathsf{softmax}(\mathbf{U}_{m})
.

During the backward propagation, error matrices are computed first. The error matrix for the output layer is defined as
\mathbf{E}_{m}=(\mathbf{A}_{m}-\mathbf{T})
, while for the remaining layers it is defined as
\mathbf{E}_{i}=(\mathbf{E}_{i+1}\circ\mathbf{W}_{i}^{T})\otimes\mathsf{dReLU}(\mathbf{U}_{i})
. Here the operation
\otimes
denotes element wise multiplication and
\mathsf{dReLU}
denotes the derivative of ReLU. This is followed by updating the weights as
\mathbf{W}_{i}=\mathbf{W}_{i}-\frac{\alpha}{B}\mathbf{A}_{i-1}^{T}\circ\mathbf{E}_{i}
.

Support Vector Machines (inference)

We consider Support Vector Machines (SVM) which is a type of supervised learning algorithm used for classification. SVM is a function which takes as input an
n
-dimensional
feature vector
,
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
, and outputs the
category
to which the feature vector belongs. SVM is implemented as a matrix
\mathbf{F}
, of dimension
q\times n
where each row of
\mathbf{F}
is called the support vector and a vector
\vec{\mathbf{b}}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{q})
, is called the
bias
. Each element of
\mathbf{F}
and
\vec{\mathbf{b}}
lies in
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{\ell}}
. Each support vector along with a scalar from the bias can classify the input
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
into a specific category. More precisely, let
\mathbf{F}_{i}
denote the
i^{\text{th}}
row of matrix
\mathbf{F}
. Then, the value
\mathbf{F}_{i}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{x}}+b_{i}
specifies how likely
\vec{\mathbf{x}}
is to be in category
i
. To find the most likely category, we compute argmax over these values, i.e.
\text{category}(\vec{\mathbf{x}})=\text{argmax}_{i\in\{1,\ldots,q\}}\mathbf{F}_{i}\cdot\vec{\mathbf{x}}+b_{i}
.

Network architectures

We consider the following networks for benchmarking. These are chosen based on the different range of model parameters and layers used in the network. We refer readers to
[
134
]
for a detailed architecture of the neural networks.

1.

SVM:
This consists of 10 categories for classification
[
50
]
.

2.

NN-1:
This is a fully connected network with 3 layers with
\mathsf{ReLU}
activation after each layer. This network has around
118
K parameters and is chosen from
[
101
,
110
]
.

3.

NN-2:
This is a convolutional neural network comprising of 2 hidden layers, with 100 and 10 nodes
[
120
,
101
,
38
]
.

4.

NN-3:
This network, called LeNet
[
91
]
, comprises of
2
convolutional layers and
2
fully connected layers with ReLU activation after each layer, additionally followed by maxpool for convolutional layers. This network has approximately
431
K parameters.

5.

NN-4:
This network, called VGG16
[
127
]
, was the runner-up of ILSVRC-2014 competition. This network has
16
layers in total and contains fully-connected, convolutional, ReLU activation and maxpool layers. This network has about
138
million parameters.

Datasets.

To benchmark the machine learning algorithms, we use the following real-world datasets:

–

MNIST
[
90
]
is a collection of
28\times 28
pixel, handwritten digit images with a label between
0
and
9
for each. It has
60,000
and respectively,
10,000
images in training and test set. We evaluate Linear Regression, Logistic Regression, NN-1, NN-3 and SVM on this dataset.

–

CIFAR-10
[
88
]
has
32\times 32
pixel images of
10
different classes such as dogs, horses, etc. It has
50,000
images for training and
10,000
for testing, with
6000
images in each class. We evaluate NN-2, NN-4 on this dataset.

Benchmarking Environment Details

The protocols are benchmarked over a Wide Area Network (WAN), instantiated using n1-standard-64 instances of Google Cloud
1
1

1

https://cloud.google.com/
, with machines located in East Australia (
P_{0}
), South Asia (
P_{1}
), South East Asia (
P_{2}
), and West Europe (
P_{3}
). The machines are equipped with 2.0 GHz Intel (R) Xeon (R) (Skylake) processors supporting hyper-threading, with 64 vCPUs, and 240 GB of RAM Memory. Parties are connected by pairwise authenticated bidirectional synchronous channels (eg. instantiated via TLS over TCP/IP). We use a limited bandwidth of
40
MBps between every pair of parties and the average round-trip time (
\mathsf{rtt}
)
2
2

2

Time for communicating 1 KB of data between a pair of parties
values among the parties are

For a fair comparison, we implemented and benchmarked all the protocols, including the protocols of SecureML
[
102
]
and ABY3
[
101
]
, building on the ENCRYPTO library
[
45
]
in C++17. Primitives such as maxpool, which SecureML and ABY3 do not support, have been run using our building blocks. We would like to clarify that our code is developed for benchmarking, is not optimized for industry-grade use, and optimizations like GPU support can enhance performance. Our protocols are instantiated over a
64
-bit ring (
\mathbb{Z}_{2^{64}}
), and the collision-resistant hash function is instantiated using SHA-256. We use multi-threading, and our machines are capable of handling a total of 64 threads. Each experiment is run 10 times, and the average values are reported. We use
1
KB =
8192
bits and use a batch size of
B=128
for training.

Benchmarking Parameters

We evaluate the protocols across a variety of parameters as given in Table
10.2
. In addition to parameters such as runtime, communication, and
online throughput
(
\mathsf{TP}
)
[
7
,
8
,
101
,
38
]
, the cumulative runtime (sum of the up-time of all the hired servers) is also reported. This is because when deployed over third-party cloud servers, one pays for them by the communication and the uptime of the hired servers. To analyze the cost of deployment of the framework,
monetary cost
(
\sf Cost
)
[
99
]
is reported. This is done using the pricing of Google Cloud Platform
3
3

3

See https://cloud.google.com/vpc/network-pricing for network cost and https://cloud.google.com/compute/vm-instance-pricing for computation cost.
, where for
1
GB and
1
hour of usage, the costs are USD
0.08
and USD
3.04
, respectively. For protocols with an asymmetric communication graph, communication load is unevenly distributed among all the servers, leaving several communication channels underutilized. Load balancing improves the performance by running several execution threads in parallel, each with the roles of the servers changed. Load balancing has been performed in all the protocols benchmarked.

Discussion

Broadly speaking, we consider two deployment scenarios – optimized for time (
T
), and for cost (
C
). In the first one, participants want the result of the output as soon as possible while maximizing the online throughput. In the second one, they want the overall monetary cost of the system to be minimal and are willing to tolerate an overhead in the execution time.
Using multi-input multiplication gates and the 2 GC variant of the garbled makes the online phase faster but incur an increase in monetary cost. This is because they cause an overhead in communication in the preprocessing phase, and communication affects monetary cost more than uptime (in our setting).

Chapter 11
ASTRA
: 3PC Semi-honest Applications

ASTRA
T
uses multi-input multiplication gates and the 2 GC variant of the garbled world and is the fastest variant of the framework. On the other hand,
ASTRA
C
is the variant with a minimal monetary cost. We benchmark our protocols against the 3PC semi-honest framework of ABY3
[
101
]
.

11.1
ML Training

We begin with analyzing the benchmarks for linear and logistic regression.
Starting with the time-optimized variant,
ASTRA
T
is
2.5-4\times
faster than ABY3
[
101
]
in online runtime for training.
For linear regression, this reduction is observed due to the different
\mathsf{rtt}
s among the three parties. This difference vanishes if
\mathsf{rtt}
between every pair of parties is the same. However, the reduction in the online run time for the case of logistic regression is primarily due to the round-optimized bit extraction circuit. Specifically, we use the depth-optimized bit extraction circuit while instantiating the sigmoid activation function using multi-input AND gates.
We observe a reduction of up to
2\times
in communication (
{\sf Comm}_{\sf tot}
) in
ASTRA
T
over ABY3. This is due to the extra cost required for performing truncation in ABY3.
These reductions in communication and run time, coupled with the requirement of one less party in the online phase, directly impact the monetary cost of the system, where
ASTRA
T
brings in a saving of up to
78\%
over ABY3.
On the other hand, the cost-optimized variant
ASTRA
C
is around
1.5\times
slower in the online phase than
ASTRA
T
. However, it is still faster than ABY3 due to the reason discussed above. Further, this variant has
1.3\times
lesser communication cost compared to
ASTRA
T
.

For neural networks,
ASTRA

is up to
3.6\times
faster than ABY3 in the online phase, similar to the observation in logistic regression.
Concerning the communication,
ASTRA
T
has a slightly higher communication than ABY3 for smaller NNs. However, the gap closes for larger NNs. This phenomenon is observed because of the trade-off in the increase in communication due to the use of multi-input multiplication versus the reduction in communication due to the free truncation operation. However, the cost-optimized variant,
ASTRA
C
, has a better communication cost than ABY3. Further,
ASTRA
C
is up to
1.4\times
slower than
ASTRA
T
in terms of online run time, while it is better than ABY3. Note that the requirement of one less party in the online phase coupled with the improvements in communication and run time results in saving up to
87\%
in the monetary cost of

C
over ABY3, and up to
18\%
over

. As the depth increases, we observe that the gap in the monetary cost of
ASTRA
C
and

closes in.

11.2
ML Inference

A similar trend for linear and logistic regression inference is observed for training, where both
ASTRA
T
and
ASTRA
C
outperform ABY3. The exception concerns the slightly higher communication of
ASTRA
T
compared to ABY3 due to the higher communication cost required for multi-input multiplication gates. This difference, however, vanishes for larger circuits, as will be evident from Table
11.2
.
For neural networks, the time-optimized variant
ASTRA
T
is faster when it comes to online run time (
{\sf PT}_{\sf on}
), by
4.4\times
over ABY3. This is also reflected in the
\mathsf{TP}
, where the improvement is up to
2.8\times
, as evident from Figure
11.1(c)
. For inference, the communication is in the order of a few megabytes, while run time is in the order of a few seconds. The key observation is that communication is well suited for the bandwidth used (40 MBps). So unlike training, the monetary cost in inference depends more on run time rather than on communication. This is evident from Table
11.2
which shows that
ASTRA
T
saves on monetary cost up to a factor of
4
over ABY3. A similar trend is observed in the case of Support Vector Machines.

Note that the cost-optimized variant underperforms in terms of monetary cost compared to
ASTRA

. This is because run time plays a more significant role in monetary cost than communication. Hence for inference, the time-optimized variant becomes the optimal choice.

11.3.2
Comparison operations

Table
11.5
compares the performance of the frameworks for circuits of varying depth. At each layer of the circuits, we perform 128 comparisons where the comparison results are generated in arithmetic shared form. The idea is that each layer emulates a comparison layer in an NN with a batch size of 128.

†
†
nicematrix-placeholder:
NiceTabular (nicematrix)

Table 11.5
:
Benchmarking of comparisons over various depths. Each of the layer has 128 comparisons. Time is reported in minutes, and monetary cost in USD.

To summarise the experimental results, beyond a depth of roughly 100, the time-optimized variant (
ASTRA
T
) starts outperforming in every metric, especially monetary cost, over the cost-optimized one (
ASTRA
C
). This is because as the depth increases, runtime (
CT
) grows at a much higher rate than the total communication. What we can infer from Table
11.5
is that if one were to use a DNN with a depth of over 100,
ASTRA
T
becomes the optimal choice.

Chapter 12
SWIFT
: 3PC Fair and Robust Applications

SWIFT
T
uses multi-input multiplication gates and the 2 GC variant of the garbled world and is the fastest variant of the framework. On the other hand,
SWIFT
C
is the variant with a minimal monetary cost. We report only the numbers for the fair variant of
SWIFT
and not the robust variant since the overhead of robust over its fair counterpart is very minimal for the algorithms considered in this thesis.

12.1
ML Training

We begin with analyzing the benchmarks for linear and logistic regression. The improvements observed in the three-party semi-honest case carry forward to
SWIFT
as well. Both
SWIFT
T
and
SWIFT
C
showcase an improvement over ABY3 in terms of communication and run time. This also improves the monetary cost over ABY3, where the saving is up to
70\%
. One of the primary reasons for the improvement is the reduction in communication. This is attributed to an improved dot product protocol whose communication cost is independent of the vector dimension and a method for truncation which does not incur any overhead in the online phase. Moreover, our multiplication protocol has around
3.5\times
improvement in terms of communication over ABY3.

Like
ASTRA
, the cost-optimized variant,
SWIFT
C
, saves
15\%
in monetary cost over
SWIFT
T
, while incurring the overhead of
1.2\times
in the online run time.

Chapter 13
Tetrad
: 4PC Fair and Robust Applications

Tetrad
T
uses multi-input multiplication gates and the 2 GC variant of the garbled world and is the fastest variant of the framework. On the other hand,
Tetrad
C
is the variant with a minimal monetary cost. We report only the numbers for the fair variant of
Tetrad
and not the robust variant since the overhead of robust over its fair counterpart is very minimal for the algorithms considered in this thesis.

For training, we benchmark against the fair 4PC framework of Trident
[
38
]
. For inference, in addition to Trident, we also benchmark against the 4PC robust protocol of SWIFT
[
85
]
since it supports NN inference. Note that the best case performance of Fantastic Four
[
46
]
, when cast in the preprocessing model, resembles that of SWIFT. In contrast, their worst-case execution (3PC malicious) is an order of magnitude slower (cf. §
5.2.6.1
), as demonstrated in their paper (cf. Table 2 of
[
46
]
).

13.1
ML Training

Starting with the time-optimized variant,
Tetrad
T
is
3-4\times
faster than Trident in online runtime.
The primary factor is the reduction in online rounds of our protocol due to multi-input gates. More precisely, we use the depth-optimized bit extraction circuit while instantiating the ReLU activation function using multi-input AND gates (cf. §
9.1.2
). Looking at the total communication (
{\sf Comm}_{\sf tot}
) in Table
13.2
, we observe that the gap in
{\sf Comm}_{\sf tot}
between
Tetrad
T
vs. Trident decreases as the networks get deeper. This is justified as the improvement in communication of our dot product with truncation outpaces the overhead in communication caused by multi-input gates. The impact of this is more pronounced with NN-4, as observed by the lower monetary cost of
Tetrad
T
over Trident.
Another reason is that there are two active parties (
P_{1},P_{2}
) in our framework, whereas Trident has three. Given the allocation of servers, the best
\mathsf{rtt}
Trident can get with three parties
(P_{0},P_{1},P_{2})
is
153.74ms
, compared to
62.01ms
of Tetrad, contributing to Tetrad being faster. However, if the
\mathsf{rtt}
among all the parties were similar, this gap would be closed. Concretely, the online runtime (
{\sf PT_{on}}
) of Trident will be similar to that of
Tetrad
C
.

†

13.2
ML Inference

Similar to training, the time-optimized variant for inference is faster when it comes to
{\sf PT}_{\sf on}
, by
4-6\times
over Trident. This is also reflected in the
\mathsf{TP}
, where the improvement is about
2.8-5.5\times
, as evident from Figure
13.1(c)
. In inference, the communication is in the order of megabytes, while run time is in the order of a few seconds. The key observation is that communication is well suited for the bandwidth used (40 MBps). So unlike training, the monetary cost in inference depends more on run time rather than on communication. This is evident from Table
13.5
which shows that
Tetrad
T
saves on monetary cost up to a factor of
10
over Trident.

Note that the cost-optimized variant underperforms in terms of monetary cost compared to
Tetrad
T
. This is because, as mentioned earlier, run time plays a more significant role in monetary cost than communication. Hence for inference, the time-optimized variant becomes the optimal choice.

Chapter 14
ABY2.0
: 2PC Semi-honest Applications

ABY2.0
T
makes use of multi-input multiplication gates and is the fastest variant of the framework. On the other hand,
ABY2.0
C
is the variant with a minimal monetary cost. We benchmark our protocols against the 2PC semi-honest framework of SecureML
[
102
]
. The preprocessing phase of
ABY2.0
is similar to SecureML except for the use of multi-input multiplication in
ABY2.0
T
. The preprocessing can be performed either using oblivious transfer or via homomorphic encryption as discussed in Chapter
6
. Note that the benchmarking is performed only for the online phase.

14.1
ML Training

Starting with the time-optimized variant,
ABY2.0
T
is up to two orders of magnitude faster than SecureML
[
102
]
in run time as well as communication.
The reduction is primarily due to the following: (i) the improved dot product protocol whose online phase communication is independent of the dimension of the vector, and (ii) improvements in online rounds due to multi-input multiplication.
These reductions in communication and run time directly impact the monetary cost of the system, where
ABY2.0
T
brings in a saving of up to
342\times
over SecureML.
On the other hand, the cost-optimized variant
ABY2.0
C
is around
1.3\times
slower than
ABY2.0
T
. However, it is still faster than SecureML due to the reasons discussed above. Further, this variant has a slightly higher communication than
ABY2.0
T
due to the absence of multi-input multiplication.

Chapter 15
Conclusion and Open Problems

This thesis designed
{\bf MPCLeague}
, a robust MPC platform for privacy-preserving machine learning applications. The focus was on the small-party setting of two, three and four parties with at most one corruption under the control of a monolithic static adversary. A unified protocol design was presented, focusing on practical efficiency, which outperforms the state-of-the-art protocols by several orders of magnitude in the respective settings. On the way, several building blocks were identified for the PPML applications, and their efficient realizations were provided. Finally, the protocols were implemented by instantiating over Google Cloud instances and analyzed against various metrics such as run time, communication, throughput and monetary cost. The practicality of our platform was argued through improvements as observed in the benchmarks.

Open Problems

We leave the following problems open for further explorations.

1.

Applications:
The platform was designed for PPML applications such as linear regression, logistic regression, neural networks and support vector machines. However, other PPML applications such as graph neural networks, decision trees and random forests, quantized neural networks have not been explored much in the literature. Extending our platform to provide support for these advanced applications is an interesting direction. This may require support for new building blocks in layer II, such as square-root, exponentiation, batch normalization, to name a few. While the platform discussed PPML applications, it is worthwhile to explore non-PPML applications such as private-set intersection, private-information retrieval, genome sequence matching.

2.

Adversarial setting:
The focus of the thesis was primarily on the honest majority setting. A step towards a dishonest majority was also taken, albeit in the semi-honest two-party setting. It is an interesting question to explore the dishonest majority setting in the presence of a malicious adversary. While protocols were designed in the synchronous network model with static corruptions, designing protocols in the asynchronous network model and against a stronger adaptive adversary is left open.

The recent notion of Friends-and-Foes
[
4
]
(FaF) security resembles real-world corruption more closely, where the honest parties are instead considered to be semi-honest. Our protocols do not adhere to this security notion, and designing protocols for the same is an interesting future direction.
Finally, our protocols, together with the above-mentioned adversarial settings, can be explored for the general
n
-party case.

3.

Federated Learning:
The advancements in PPML have paved the way for federated learning which allows collaborative training while ensuring the training data resides only with the data owners. Since the data does not leave its owner, it increases the trust in the system and has gained a lot of attention recently. The traditional approach of realizing PPML via MPC does not extend naively to the federated setting. We leave open the question of realizing our architecture in the federated setting as an open problem.
